6
Discussion

We finalize with some additional discussions complementing our contributions already highlighted in Section
3
. The
\ell_{1}
norm treats each variable in the same manner. Alternatively, the Slope norm adjusts the weight according to the variable’s magnitude. The papers
[
11
,
7
]
propose and study the Slope norm as a finer
regularization
norm for sparse linear regression. Indeed,
[
7
]
shows that sparse linear regression with Slope regularization achieves the
exact
optimal rate

\sqrt{s\log(ep/s)/n}
adaptively to
s
, a property not satisfied by the
\ell_{1}
-norm. In this work, we exploit the Slope norm for a related but considerably different purpose: to construct a finer
robust loss
against label contamination (see Definition
2.1
). Differently than classical Huber regression, the “Sorted Huber’s loss” in Definition
2.1
assign, for each
i
th data point, higher penalization to higher outlier levels. One message of the present work is that
M
-estimation with the Sorted Huber’s loss seem to have theoretical improvements compared to Huber regression: it achieves the
subgaussian
optimal rate in response adversarial robust regression (up to a log factor in
1/\epsilon
) with a breakdown point
\epsilon\leq c
for a constant
c\in(0,1)
, adaptively to
(\epsilon,d_{\mbox{\tiny eff}},\delta)
, where
d_{\mbox{\tiny eff}}
denotes the “effective” dimension and
\delta
is the failure probability. This property holds despite the parsimony structure of the parameter (sparsity or low-rankness). We also show that these rates are valid
uniformly
on the failure probability
\delta
, i.e., the tuning of our estimators do not depend on
\delta
. Although the details are not presented explicitly, it is clear that, in
low-dimensions
(
n\geq d_{1}d_{2}
), subgaussian optimality is also achieved in least-squares regression with the Sorted Huber’s loss. Our simulations also shown in practice that Sorted Huber regression can outperform classical Huber regression (Section
5
, Figure
1
(b)).

The Huber loss is a seminal and fundamental robust loss which has been considered in numerous works under various different perspectives
[
72
,
78
,
4
,
38
,
6
]
. One fundamental open question would be to further compare Huber regression with Sorted Huber regression proposed in Definition
2.1
with respect to other statistical metrics considered in the literature. This would be interesting on the theoretical level and with further numerical study. One example would be to give a tight comparison between the breakdown points of both losses, at least in the Gaussian set-up.

To the best of our knowledge, there seems to be no prior estimation theory for the trace-regression problem with matrix decomposition (Theorem
4.3
). It is interesting to remark that our analysis of this problem crucially makes use of a concentration inequality for the Product Process. This seems to be a new application in the context of matrix decomposition theory
[
14
,
2
]
. In this paper we are mainly concerned with the noisy setting. In parallel with the established theory of signal recovery in Compressed Sensing, it would be interesting to investigate further when
exact
recovery is possible in this context. This probably would require different set of assumptions such as the
incoherence
condition
[
15
]
. Other analytic tools from nonconvex optimization
[
20
]
and the corresponding corrupted model are also worthy of investigation.

Web-based supporting materials for “Outlier-robust sparse/low-rank least-squares regression and robust matrix completion”

7
Basic notation

Throughout the paper, given
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
A^{(\ell)}:=A/\sqrt{\ell}
whenever
A
is a number, vector or function. Regarding the tuning parameters
(\lambda,\tau)
, we will sometimes use the definition

\gamma:=\lambda/\tau
. With respect to the Slope norm in

\mathbb{R}^{n}
with sequence

\boldsymbol{\omega}=(\omega_{i})_{i\in[n]}
given by
\omega_{i}=\sqrt{\log(An/i)}
for some
A\geq 2
, we set

\Omega:=\{\sum_{i=1}^{o}\omega_{i}^{2}\}^{1/2}
. Note that from the Stirling formula,
\Omega\asymp o\log(n/o)

[
7
]
. Throughout the paper,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}:=\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}^{*}
,

\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}
and

\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
. It will be useful to define the map

\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{B};\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B})+\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{\theta}.

Letting
\Pi
be the distribution of
\mathbf{X}
, we define the bilinear form

\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}:=\mathbb{E}[\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle],

and, as before, the

L^{2}(\Pi)
-pseudo-distance

\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}:=\{\mathbb{E}[\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{X}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}^{2}]\}^{1/2}.

We denote by
\mathfrak{S}
the covariance operator of
\mathbf{X}
, that is, the self-adjoint linear operator on

\mathds{R}^{p}
satisfying

\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{V}),\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}
for all
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}
. It will be useful to define the pseudo-norms

\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}:=\{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}\}^{1/2}
on
\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\|[\mathbf{V};\mathbf{W}]\|_{\Pi}:=\{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}^{2}+\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\Pi}^{2}\}^{1/2}
on
(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
.

We define the unit balls

\mathbb{B}_{\Pi}:=\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\leq 1\},

\mathbb{B}_{F}:=\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{V}\|_{F}\leq 1\},

and

\mathbb{B}_{\ell}^{k}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\ell}\leq 1\}.

With a slight abuse of notation, we denote by

\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}}:=\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\leq 1\}

and

\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}:=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u})\leq 1\}

the correspondent unit balls for norms
\mathcal{R}
on

\mathds{R}^{p}
and
\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. All the corresponding unit spheres will take the symbol
\mathbb{S}
.

Finally, the
Gaussian width
of a compact set

\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times\ell}
is the quantity

\mathscr{G}(\mathcal{B}):=\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{B}}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{\Xi}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle],

where
\boldsymbol{\Xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times\ell}
is random matrix with iid
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries.

8
Proofs of Theorems
4.1
and
4.2

Recall estimator (
7
).

Throughout Sections
8.1
and
8.2
,

\|\cdot\|_{\Pi}
can be regarded as a generic pseudo-norm.
Moreover,
\{(\mathbf{X}_{i},\xi_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
satisfying (
3
) can be regarded as deterministic with

\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{i})_{i\in[n]}
. As before,

\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{M}
are the design and augmented design operators associated to the sequence
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Probabilistic assumptions are used only in Sections
8.3
,
8.4
and
8.5
.

8.1
Design properties, cones and restricted eigenvalues

Next we present some structural properties for the design operator

\mathfrak{X}:\mathds{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 8.1

(Transfer principles)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a norm over
\mathds{R}^{p}
,
\mathcal{Q}
be a norm over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and subsets
\mathbb{C}\subset\mathds{R}^{p}
and

\mathbb{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(i)

Given positive numbers
\mathsf{a}_{1}
and

\mathsf{a}_{2}
, we say that
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies

\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1};\mathsf{a}_{2})
on
\mathbb{C}
if

(51)

\displaystyle\forall\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{C},\quad\big\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\big\|_{2}\geq\mathsf{a}_{1}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}-\mathsf{a}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V}).

(ii)

Given positive numbers
\mathsf{b}_{1}
,
\mathsf{b}_{2}
and
\mathsf{b}_{3}
, we say that
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies

\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1};\mathsf{b}_{2};\mathsf{b}_{3})
if

(52)

\displaystyle\forall[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathds{R}^{p},\quad|\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\rangle|\leq\mathsf{b}_{1}\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{3}\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\Pi}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}).

(iii)

Given positive numbers

\mathsf{c}_{1}
,
\mathsf{c}_{2}
and
\mathsf{c}_{3}
, we say that
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{c}_{1};\mathsf{c}_{2};\mathsf{c}_{3})
on
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
if

(53)

\displaystyle\forall[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathbb{C}^{\prime},\quad\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})+\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\geq\mathsf{c}_{1}\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}-\mathsf{c}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})-\mathsf{c}_{3}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}).

(iv)

Given positive numbers

\mathsf{f}_{1}
,
\mathsf{f}_{2}
and
\mathsf{f}_{3}
, we will say that
(\mathfrak{X},\boldsymbol{\xi})
satisfies
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2};\mathsf{f}_{3})
if

(54)

\displaystyle\forall[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathds{R}^{p},\quad|\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})+\boldsymbol{u}\rangle|\leq\mathsf{f}_{1}\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})+\mathsf{f}_{3}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}).

If either
\mathbb{C}=\mathds{R}^{p}
or
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}=\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we omit the reference to the set where the above properties hold.

\TP
is essentially “restricted strong convexity”
[
61
]
, a well known fundamental property in high-dimensional estimation. Indeed,
\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1};0)
on

\mathbb{C}
is strong convexity of
\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}
on

\mathbb{C}
with respect to the pseudo-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Pi}
.
4
4

4

To be precise,
\mathsf{a}_{1}
is an absolute constant for general classes of designs. The usual notion of restricted eigenvalue, as e.g. in
[
10
,
7
]
, is with respect to the Frobenius norm. In that case, the eigenvalue constant represents a “condition number”. In this sense, it is more precise to say that
\TP
is a relaxation of Bernstein’s condition.

\MP(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2};0)
implies a bound on the “multiplier process”
\mathbf{V}\mapsto\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle
, also an essential property used in high-dimensional estimation. In this work, we give improved bounds on the MP property regarding the confidence level.

Remark 4
.

We make use of the term “transfer principle” or “reduction principle” making homage to the terms coined in
[
8
,
6
,
7
]
.

The “augmented” notions of
\IP
,
\ATP
and
\MP

will be useful for robust linear regression. From the next deterministic lemma,

\ATP
is a consequence of
\TP
and
\IP
. We will show in Section
8.3
that
\TP
,
\IP
and
\MP
are satisfied by subgaussian designs with high probability.

Lemma 8.2

(
\TP+\IP\Rightarrow\ATP
, Lemma 7 in
[
29
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a norm over
\mathds{R}^{p}
and
\mathcal{Q}
a norm over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with unit ball
\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{n}
. Suppose
\mathfrak{X}:\mathds{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies

\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1};\mathsf{a}_{2})

and
\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1};\mathsf{b}_{2};\mathsf{b}_{3})
for some positive numbers
\mathsf{a}_{1}
,
\mathsf{a}_{2}
,

\mathsf{b}_{1}
,
\mathsf{b}_{2}
and
\mathsf{b}_{3}
. Then, for any

\alpha>0
,

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies the
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{c}_{1};\mathsf{c}_{2};\mathsf{c}_{3})
with constants

\mathsf{c}_{1}=\{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}-\mathsf{b}_{1}-\alpha^{2}\}^{1/2}
,
\mathsf{c}_{2}=\mathsf{a}_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{2}/\alpha

and
\mathsf{c}_{3}=\mathsf{b}_{3}/\alpha
. Taking
\alpha=\mathsf{a}_{1}/2
, we obtain that

\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{c}_{1};\mathsf{c}_{2};\mathsf{c}_{3})
holds with constants

\mathsf{c}_{1}=\{(3/4)\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}-\mathsf{b}_{1}\}^{1/2}
,
\mathsf{c}_{2}=\mathsf{a}_{2}+2\mathsf{b}_{2}/\mathsf{a}_{1}
and
\mathsf{c}_{3}=2\mathsf{b}_{3}/\mathsf{a}_{1}
.

We now recall the definition of decomposable norms
[
70
,
55
]
.

Definition 8.3

(Decomposable norm)
.

A norm
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathds{R}^{p}
is said to be decomposable if for all
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
, there exist linear map
\mathbf{V}\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V})
such that, for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
, defining

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}):=\mathbf{V}-\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V})
,

•

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{B})=0
,

•

\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}),\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V})\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=0
,

•

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))+\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))
.

In particular,
\|\mathbf{V}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V})\|_{F}^{2}+\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V})\|_{F}^{2}
.
For
\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}\setminus\{0\}
, we define

\Psi_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{V}):=\frac{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})}{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{F}}.

We omit the subscript
\mathcal{R}
when it is clear in the context.

Two well known examples of decomposable norms are the
\ell_{1}
and nuclear norms.

Example

(
\ell_{1}
-norm)
.

Given
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
with
sparsity support

\mathscr{S}(\mathbf{B}):=\{[j,k]:\mathbf{B}_{j,k}\neq 0\}
, the
\ell_{1}
-norm in
\mathds{R}^{p}
satisfies the above decomposability condition with the map

\mathbf{V}\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}):=\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{B})^{c}}

where
\mathbf{V}_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{B})^{c}}
denotes the
d_{1}\times d_{2}
matrix whose entries are zero at indexes in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbf{B})
.

Example

(Nuclear norm)
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
with rank
r:=\rank(\mathbf{B})
, singular values
\{\sigma_{j}\}_{j\in[r]}
and singular vector decomposition
\mathbf{B}=\sum_{j\in[r]}\sigma_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}
. Here
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{j}\}_{j\in[r]}
are the left singular vectors spanning the subspace
\mathcal{U}
and
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\}_{j\in[r]}
are the right singular vectors spanning the subspace
\mathcal{V}
. The pair
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V})
is sometimes referred as the
low-rank support
of
\mathbf{B}
. Given subspace
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
let

\mathbf{P}_{S^{\perp}}
denote the matrix defining the orthogonal projection onto
S^{\perp}
. Then, the map

\mathbf{V}\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}):=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}^{\perp}}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\perp}}^{\top}

satisfy the decomposability condition for the nuclear norm
\|\cdot\|_{N}
.

Decomposability is particularly useful because of the following well known lemmas
[
70
,
7
]
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a decomposable norm over
\mathds{R}^{p}
. Let
\mathbf{B},\hat{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
and

\mathbf{V}:=\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}
. Then, for any
\nu\in[0,1]
,

(55)

\displaystyle\nu\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})+\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\leq(1+\nu)\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))-(1-\nu)\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V})).

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
o\in[n]
,
\boldsymbol{\theta},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{0}\leq o
. Set

\boldsymbol{u}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}
. Then

\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{o}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}-\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}.

In particular, for any
\nu\in[0,1]
,

(56)

\displaystyle\nu\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\sharp}+\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\leq(1+\nu)\sum_{i=1}^{o}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}-(1-\nu)\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}.

Finally, we state some cone definitions and recall the definition of restricted eigenvalue constant.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a decomposable norm over
\mathds{R}^{p}
. Given
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
and
c_{0},\gamma,\eta>0
, we define the following cones

(57)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\mathcal{R}}(c_{0})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\mathbf{V}:\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V}))\leq c_{0}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))\right\},

(58)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\mathcal{R}}(c_{0},\gamma,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\left\{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]:\gamma\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V}))+\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}\leq c_{0}\left[\gamma\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))+\eta\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\right]\right\}.

We will omit the subscript
\mathcal{R}
when the norm is clear in the context.

Definition 8.7

(Restricted eigenvalue)
.

Given a convex cone
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathds{R}^{p}
, we define

\mu(\mathbb{C}):=\sup_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{C}}\frac{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{F}}{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}}.

8.2
Deterministic bounds

Throughout this section
\mathcal{R}
is a general decomposable norm on
\mathds{R}^{p}
(see Definition
8.3
).

Lemma 8.8

(Dimension reduction)
.

Suppose

(i)

(\mathfrak{X},\boldsymbol{\xi})
satisfies the
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2};\mathsf{f}_{3})

for some positive numbers
\mathsf{f}_{1}
,
\mathsf{f}_{2}
and
\mathsf{f}_{3}
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies the
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{c}_{1};\mathsf{c}_{2};\mathsf{c}_{3})
for some positive numbers
\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{3}
.

(iii)

\lambda=\gamma\tau\geq 2[\mathsf{f}_{2}+(\nicefrac{{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{2}}}{{\mathsf{c}_{1}}})],\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\geq 2[\mathsf{f}_{3}+(\nicefrac{{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{3}}}{{\mathsf{c}_{1}}})].

Then either
[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(6,\gamma,\Omega)
or

(59)

\displaystyle\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\bigg)\frac{28\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{3}},

(60)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{28\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

Proof.

The first order condition of (
7
) at

[\hat{\mathbf{B}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}]
is equivalent to the statement: there exist

\mathbf{V}\in\partial\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})
and
\boldsymbol{u}\in\partial\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}
such that for all
[\mathbf{B};\boldsymbol{\theta}]
,

(61)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}\left[y_{i}^{(n)}-\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{B}})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\right]\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}^{(n)}_{i},\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\lambda\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle,

(62)

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{y}^{(n)}-\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\tau\langle\boldsymbol{u},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle.

Evaluating at
[\mathbf{B}^{*};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}]
and using that

\boldsymbol{y}^{(n)}=\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}^{*})+\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}

we obtain

(63)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}\left[\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}_{i}\left(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\right)+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{i}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\right]\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}^{(n)}_{i},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[n]}\xi_{i}^{(n)}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i}^{(n)},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle-\lambda\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle,

(64)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}\left(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\right)+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}-\tau\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\right\rangle,

so summing both equations we get

(65)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle-\lambda\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle-\tau\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\rangle.

There is
\mathbf{U}
such that

\mathcal{R}^{*}(\mathbf{U})\leq 1
and
\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{U},\hat{\mathbf{B}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})
.
5
5

5

Recall the subdifferential of a norm
\mathcal{R}
at a point
\mathbf{W}
is
\partial\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{W})=\{\mathbf{U}:\mathcal{R}^{*}(\mathbf{U})\leq 1,\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{W})\}
.
Hence, we get

-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{B}^{*}-\hat{\mathbf{B}},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{B}^{*},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\leq\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}).

Similarly,
-\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},\boldsymbol{u}\rangle\leq\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}
.
From these bounds we obtain

(66)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(i)}}{{\leq}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\mathsf{f}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

(67)

\displaystyle+\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big)+\tau\big(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\big)

(68)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}+\left(\mathsf{f}_{2}+\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\right)\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\left(\mathsf{f}_{3}+\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

(69)

\displaystyle+\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big)+\tau\big(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\big)

(70)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(iii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}+(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+(\nicefrac{{\tau}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

(71)

\displaystyle+\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big)+\tau\big(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\big)

(72)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}+\triangle,

where in last inequality we used the decomposability of
\mathcal{R}
and Lemmas
8.4
-
8.5
with
\nu:=1/2
and have defined

(73)

\displaystyle\triangle:=(\nicefrac{{3\lambda}}{{2}})(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}})(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})-(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}^{\perp})(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+(\nicefrac{{3\tau\Omega}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}-(\nicefrac{{\tau}}{{2}})\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})_{i}^{\sharp}.

Define also
G:=\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}
and
H:=(\nicefrac{{3\lambda}}{{2}})(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}})(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+(\nicefrac{{3\tau\Omega}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}
. We split the argument in two cases.

Case 1:

\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}G\leq H
. In that case, from (
72
) we obtain

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(6,\gamma,\Omega)
.

Case 2:

\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}G\geq H
. In that case we obtain
G^{2}\leq\frac{2\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}G\Rightarrow G\leq\frac{2\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}
. Therefore
H\leq 2\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}
. We first establish a bound on
\gamma\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}
. Again from (
72
), we obtain that

(74)

\displaystyle\lambda(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}^{\perp})(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})_{i}^{\sharp}\leq 4\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}G\leq 8\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

This fact and decomposability imply

(75)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}\leq\frac{2}{3}H+8\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\leq\frac{28\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

From the
\ATP
in
\rm{(ii)}
and the above bound,

(76)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}\leq\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}+\mathsf{c}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\mathsf{c}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}\leq 2\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}+\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\bigg)\frac{28\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2
.

Suppose that, in addition to (i)-(iii) in Lemma
8.8
, the following condition holds:

(iv)

For
R:=\Psi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(12))
, assume

14\big({\mathsf{c}_{2}}\vee\gamma\mathsf{c}_{3}\big)\left(R^{2}+\frac{4\Omega^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)^{1/2}\leq\mathsf{c}_{1}.

Then either (
59
)-(
60
) hold or

(77)

\displaystyle\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}+\frac{6}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\sqrt{\lambda^{2}R^{2}+4\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}},

(78)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\big\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{56}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}+\frac{56}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\frac{196}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}.

Proof.

From Lemma
8.8
, we only need to consider the case when
[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(6,\gamma,\Omega)
. A slight variation of the argument to establish (
72
) leads to

(79)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mathsf{f}_{1}\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}+\triangle,

where
\triangle
was already defined in the proof of Lemma
8.8
.
\ATP
as stated in item
\rm{(ii)}
of Lemma
8.8
further leads to

(80)

\displaystyle\left\{\mathsf{c}_{1}\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}-\mathsf{c}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})-\mathsf{c}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}\right\}_{+}\leq\sqrt{\mathsf{f}_{1}\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}+\triangle}.

We now split our arguments in two cases.

Case 1:

12\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))\geq\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))
. Hence

\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(12)
. Decomposability of
\mathcal{R}
and

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(6,\gamma,\Omega)
further imply

(81)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\mathsf{c}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\bigg)\big(\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp})

(82)

\displaystyle\leq 7\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\bigg)\big(\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))+\tau\Omega\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2})

(83)

\displaystyle\leq 7\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\bigg)\bigg(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}.

Similarly,

(84)

\displaystyle\triangle

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{3\lambda}}{{2}})\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))+(\nicefrac{{3\tau\Omega}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}

(85)

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})\bigg(\lambda^{2}\Psi^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))\mu^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(12))+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}.

To ease notation, define
x=\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}
,

(86)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=7\big(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\big)\big(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\big)^{1/2},

(87)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\mathsf{f}_{1}+(\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})\big(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

From (
80
), (
83
) and (
85
) we get

(88)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}x\leq Ax+\sqrt{Bx}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x\leq\frac{B}{(\mathsf{c}_{1}-A)^{2}}

provided that
{A\leq\mathsf{c}_{1}}
. Assuming
{2A\leq\mathsf{c}_{1}}
, we get

(89)

\displaystyle\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}\leq\frac{4B}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}=\frac{4}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}+\frac{6}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2})^{1/2}.

For deriving the bound on
\gamma\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}
, we again use the decomposability of
\mathcal{R}
and
[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(6,\gamma,\Omega)
, obtaining

(90)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\leq(7\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))+6\tau\Omega\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2})

(91)

\displaystyle\leq 7\bigg(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}

(92)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{28}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\bigg(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{42}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\bigg(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\bigg)

(93)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{56}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}^{2}_{1}+\frac{56}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\bigg(\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}\bigg).

Case 2:

12\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))<\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))
. As

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}};\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(6,\gamma,\Omega)
, we get

(94)

\displaystyle 6\gamma\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))+\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})^{\sharp}_{i}\leq 6\Omega\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}.

This and decomposability of
\mathcal{R}
imply

(95)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\mathsf{c}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\Big)\big(\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp})

(96)

\displaystyle\leq 7\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\bigg)\big(\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))+\tau\Omega\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2})

(97)

\displaystyle\leq 14\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\bigg)\tau\Omega\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}.

Similarly,

(98)

\displaystyle\triangle\leq(\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))+(\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})\tau\Omega\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq 3\tau\Omega\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}.

Again it is convenient to define
x=\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\,;\,\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}
,
A^{\prime}=14\big(\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}\big)\tau\Omega
and
B^{\prime}=\mathsf{f}_{1}+3\tau\Omega
. From
(
80
), (
97
) and (
98
),

(99)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}x\leq A^{\prime}x+\sqrt{B^{\prime}x}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x\leq\frac{B^{\prime}}{(\mathsf{c}_{1}-A^{\prime})^{2}}\leq\frac{4B^{\prime}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}

provided that
2A^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{c}_{1}
. In conclusion,

(100)

\displaystyle\big\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\,;\,\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\big\|_{\Pi}\leq\frac{4\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{12\tau\Omega}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}},

implying

(101)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\big\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\leq 7\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))+7\tau\Omega\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\big\|_{2}

(102)

\displaystyle\leq 14\tau\Omega\,\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\big\|_{2}

(103)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{56}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\tau\Omega+\frac{168}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}

(104)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{28}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}+\frac{196}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}.

The proof is complete by noting that the bounds in the statement of the proposition are larger than the bounds we have just established in the above cases.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

(105)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}),\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\right\rangle\leq\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\rangle+\lambda\left(2\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\right).

Proof.

As
[\hat{\mathbf{B}};\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}]
is the minimizer of (
7
), in particular

(106)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{B}}\in

\displaystyle\argmin_{\mathbf{B}}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(n)}-\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})\right\}.

The KKT conditions of the above minimization problem and the expression of the subdifferential of any norm imply that there exists
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
with

\mathcal{R}^{*}(\mathbf{V})\leq 1
and
\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\hat{\mathbf{B}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})
such that, for all
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
,

(107)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[n]}\left[\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{B}})+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-y_{i}^{(n)}\right]\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}^{(n)}_{i},\mathbf{B}-\hat{\mathbf{B}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle+\lambda\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{B}-\hat{\mathbf{B}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle

(108)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]}\left[\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\widehat{\mathbf{B}}})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{i}-\xi_{i}^{(n)}\right]\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}^{(n)}_{i},\mathbf{B}-\hat{\mathbf{B}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle+\lambda\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{B}-\hat{\mathbf{B}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle.

We can take
\mathbf{B}:=\mathbf{B}^{*}
above and obtain

(109)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq-\left\langle\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\right\rangle+\langle\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}),\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}\rangle-\lambda\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle.

Using that
\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathbf{B}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})
and
\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{B}^{*}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\leq\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})
(since
\mathcal{R}^{*}(\mathbf{V})\leq 1
), we obtain that

-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\leq\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}).

Moreover, from the triangle inequality and the decomposability property for the norm
\mathcal{R}
, one checks that

(110)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\leq\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))=2\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}).

Combining the two previous displays finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 8.10

(Trace regression)
.

Suppose the following condition holds:

(i)

(\mathfrak{X},\boldsymbol{\xi})
satisfies the
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2};\mathsf{f}_{3})

for some positive numbers
\mathsf{f}_{1}
,
\mathsf{f}_{2}
and
\mathsf{f}_{3}
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies the
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{c}_{1};\mathsf{c}_{2};\mathsf{c}_{3})
for some positive numbers
\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{3}
.

(iii)

\lambda=\gamma\tau\geq 2[\mathsf{f}_{2}+(\nicefrac{{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{2}}}{{\mathsf{c}_{1}}})],\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\geq 2[\mathsf{f}_{3}+(\nicefrac{{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{3}}}{{\mathsf{c}_{1}}})].

(iv)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies the
\IP_{\mathcal{R},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}\left(\mathsf{b}_{1};\mathsf{b}_{2};\mathsf{b}_{3}\right)
.

(v)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies the
\TP_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\mathsf{a}_{1};\mathsf{a}_{2}\right)
.

Suppose that
\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(12))<\infty
. Let
R:=\Psi(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(12))
and suppose further that

(111)

\displaystyle 14\big({\mathsf{c}_{2}}\vee\gamma\mathsf{c}_{3}\big)\left[R^{2}+\frac{4\Omega^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{c}_{1},

(112)

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}+\frac{6}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\sqrt{\lambda^{2}R^{2}+4\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}}\leq\frac{\lambda}{6\mathsf{b}_{2}}.

Define the quantities

\square:=\frac{1.5\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\lambda\mathsf{a}_{1}}\bigvee\frac{1.5\mathsf{b}_{3}}{\tau\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}},

\triangle:=\frac{\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\lambda}\bigvee\frac{\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\tau}

and

(113)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\square,\lambda R}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{56\square}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{4.5}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\right)(\lambda R)^{2}+\frac{1.5}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}(\lambda R),

(114)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\square,\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{1}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{56\square}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}+\frac{6}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{f}_{1}+\frac{4.5\mathsf{b}_{1}^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1.5\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}

(115)

\displaystyle\omega_{\square,\tau\Omega}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{196\square}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{18}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\right)(\tau\Omega)^{2}.

Then

(116)

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\Phi_{\square,\lambda R}+\Phi_{\square,\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{1}}+\omega_{\square,\tau\Omega}\right\}\bigvee\left\{2\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+\triangle\frac{28\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{3}}\right\}.

Proof.

Condition (
111
) and items
\rm{(i)}
-
\rm{(iii)}
imply that the claims of Proposition
2
hold. If (
59
)-(
60
) hold we have nothing to prove. Otherwise, (
77
)-(
78
) hold. In particular,

(117)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{f}_{1}+\frac{6}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\sqrt{\lambda^{2}R^{2}+4\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cond2:general:norm}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\lambda}{6\mathsf{b}_{2}}.

By Lemma
8.9
and
\MP
as stated in item
\rm{(i)}
,

(118)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\langle\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}),\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\rangle+\mathsf{f}_{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\lambda\left(2\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\right)

(119)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\rm iii)}}{{\leq}}-\langle\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}),\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\rangle+\mathsf{f}_{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\lambda\left(2\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\right)

(120)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\rm iv)}}{{\leq}}\mathsf{b}_{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{3}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}+\mathsf{f}_{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}+2\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\frac{\lambda}{3}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})

(121)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{b}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}-\frac{\lambda}{6}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})

(122)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cond3:general:norm}}}{{\leq}}\mathsf{b}_{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{3}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}+\mathsf{f}_{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}+2\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\frac{\lambda}{3}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}).

We now define the local variables
x:=\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\|_{\Pi}
and

(123)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{b}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}+\mathsf{f}_{1},

(124)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=\left\{2\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))-\frac{\lambda}{3}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\right\}_{+}.

On the one hand, combining the last inequality and the

\TP
, as stated in item (v), we
arrive at

(125)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{a}_{1}x-\mathsf{a}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))_{+}^{2}\leq Ax+B.

This implies that either
x\leq(\mathsf{a}_{2}/\mathsf{a}_{1})\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})
or

(126)

\displaystyle\Big(\mathsf{a}_{1}x-\mathsf{a}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})-\frac{A}{2\mathsf{a}_{1}}\Big)^{2}\leq B+\frac{A^{2}}{4\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{A\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}).

In both cases,

(127)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\frac{A}{2\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\Big\{B+\frac{A^{2}}{4\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{A\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\,\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})\Big\}^{1/2}

(128)

\displaystyle\leq 1.5\frac{\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+1.5\frac{A}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{B^{1/2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}.

On the other hand,

(129)

\displaystyle B\leq 2\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}))\leq 2\lambda Rx\leq\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{a}_{1}x}{2}+\frac{2\lambda R}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\bigg)^{2}.

Combining (
128
) and (
129
), we get

(130)

\displaystyle\frac{x}{2}\leq\frac{1.5\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\frac{1.5A}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{2\lambda R}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}.

Replacing
A
and
x
by their expressions, we arrive at

(131)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\big\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1.5\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\frac{1.5\mathsf{b}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}+1.5\mathsf{b}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}+1.5\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1.5\lambda R}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}

(132)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{1.5\mathsf{a}_{2}}{\lambda\mathsf{a}_{1}}\bigvee\frac{1.5\mathsf{b}_{3}}{\tau\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}\bigg)\big(\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}\big)+1.5\mathsf{b}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}+\frac{1.5\lambda R}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1.5\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}}.

By (
117
),

(133)

\displaystyle 1.5\mathsf{b}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq 6\frac{\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+9\frac{\mathsf{b}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}\sqrt{\lambda^{2}R^{2}+4\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}}\leq 6\frac{\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+4.5\frac{\mathsf{b}_{1}^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+4.5\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{2}+4\tau^{2}\Omega^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

The two previous inequalities and (
78
) lead to the claimed rate on
\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}\|_{\Pi}
.
∎

8.3
Properties for subgaussian
(\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol{\xi})

In this section we prove that all properties of Definition
8.1
are satisfied with high-probability.

Throughout this section, we additionally assume
(\mathbf{X},\xi)\in\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}
is a centered (not necessarily independent) random pair satisfying Assumption
3
and
\{(\mathbf{X}_{i},\xi_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
is an iid copy of
(\mathbf{X},\xi)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}
is any norm on
\mathds{R}^{p}
and
\mathcal{Q}
is any norm on

\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The proof that
L
-subgaussian designs satisfy
\TP
in Definition
8.1
will follow from a concentration result for the quadratic process due to Dirksen
[
36
]
and Bednorz
[
5
]
and, in addition, a peeling argument.

Theorem 8.11

(Theorem 5.5 in Dirksen
[
36
]
, Theorem 1 in Bednorz
[
5
]
)
.

Let
V
be a compact subset of
\mathbb{B}_{\Pi}
.

Then, for universal constant
C>0
, for any
n\geq 1
and
t>0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
,

\sup_{\mathbf{V}\in V}\left|\|\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{V})\|_{2}^{2}-n\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}^{2}\right|\leq C^{2}\left[\mathscr{G}^{2}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(V))+L\sqrt{n}(\mathscr{G}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(V)\big)+L^{2}\max(t,\sqrt{nt})\right].

Proposition 3

(
\TP
)
.

Grant the assumptions of Theorem
8.11
.

Then, for the universal constant
C>0
stated in Theorem
8.11
, for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the following property holds: for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
,

(134)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\{\left(1+\frac{C^{2}L^{2}\epsilon}{4}\right)^{1/2}-CL\sqrt{\epsilon}-\frac{CL}{\sqrt{\epsilon n}}\left[3+\sqrt{\log(18/\delta)}\right]\right\}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}

(135)

\displaystyle-1.2C\frac{\mathscr{G}\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\cap\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\mathbb{B}_{F}\right)}{\sqrt{\epsilon n}}.

In addition, for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

\delta\in(0,1]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the following property holds: for all

\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
,

(136)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\left(1-\frac{C^{2}L^{2}\epsilon}{4}\right)_{+}^{1/2}+CL\sqrt{\epsilon}+\frac{CL}{\sqrt{\epsilon n}}\left[3+\sqrt{\log(18/\delta)}\right]\right\}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}

(137)

\displaystyle+1.2C\frac{\mathscr{G}\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\cap\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\mathbb{B}_{F}\right)}{\sqrt{\epsilon n}}.

Proof.

Let
R_{1}>0
and define the set

(138)

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle:=\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}=1,\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\leq R_{1}\}.

Note that,

\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(V_{1}))\leq R_{1}\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\cap R_{1}^{-1}\mathbb{B}_{F}).

Define for convenience the function

f(r):=r\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\cap r^{-1}\mathbb{B}_{F}).

By Theorem
8.11
, there is universal
C>0
such that, for any
R_{1}>0
and

t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
,

(139)

\displaystyle 1-\inf_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{B}}\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\left[\frac{f^{2}(R_{1})}{n}+L\frac{f(R_{1})}{\sqrt{n}}+L^{2}\max\left(\frac{t}{n},\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}\right)\right].

By dividing in the cases
t\geq n
and
t\leq n
and completing the squares, the above relation implies in particular that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\delta\in(0,1/2)
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
,

(140)

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{B}}\left[\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\|_{2}-\left\{\left(1+\frac{C^{2}L^{2}\epsilon}{4}\right)^{1/2}-\sqrt{\epsilon}CL\right\}\right]\geq-C\frac{f(R_{1})}{\sqrt{\epsilon n}}-CL\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\epsilon n}}.

We use the above property and the single-parameter peeling Lemma
11.1
with constraint set
V:=\mathbb{S}_{\Pi}
, functions

M(\mathbf{V}):=\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\|_{2}-\{(1+\frac{C^{2}L^{2}\epsilon}{4})^{1/2}-\sqrt{\epsilon}CL\}
,

h(\mathbf{V}):=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})
,
g(r):=C\frac{f(r)}{\sqrt{\epsilon n}}
and constants
c:=2
and
b:=\frac{CL}{\sqrt{\epsilon n}}
. Note that the claimed inequality trivially holds if
\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}=0
by
L
-sub-Gaussianity. The desired inequality follows from Lemma
11.1
combined with the fact that

\nicefrac{{\mathbf{V}}}{{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}}}\in V,

for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
such that

\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\neq 0
and the homogeneity of norms. The proof for the upper bound is similar.
∎

We now show
\IP
in Definition
8.1
for
L
-subgaussian designs. The proof follows from Chevet’s inequality and a peeling argument in two parameters. A high-probability version of Chevet’s inequality is suggested as an exercise in Vershynin
[
1
]
. We next give a proof for completeness.

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
V
be any bounded subset of

\mathbb{B}_{\Pi}\times\mathbb{B}_{2}^{n}
Define
V_{1}:=\{\mathbf{V}:\exists\,\boldsymbol{u}\text{ s.t. }(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\in V\}

and
V_{2}:=\{\boldsymbol{u}:\exists\,\mathbf{V}\text{ s.t. }(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\in V\}
.

Then, there exists universal numerical constant
C>0
, such that, for any
n\geq 1
and
t>0
, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-t^{2})
,

\sup_{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in V}\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{V})\rangle\leq CL[\mathscr{G}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(V_{1}))+\mathscr{G}\big(V_{2}\big)+t].

Proof.

In the following, the numerical constant
C>0
may change from line to line. For each
(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\in V
, we define

(141)

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}

\displaystyle:=\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{V})\rangle=\sum_{i\in[n]}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle,\qquad W_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}:=L(\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{\Xi})\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\xi}\rangle),

where
\boldsymbol{\Xi}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are independent each one having iid
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries. Therefore,

(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\mapsto W_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}
defines a centered Gaussian process indexed by
V
.

We may easily bound the
\psi_{2}
-norm of the increments using rotation invariance of sub-Gaussian random variables. Indeed, using that
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}
is an iid sequence and Proposition 2.6.1 in
[
1
]
, there is an universal numerical constant
C>0
such that, given
[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]
and
[\mathbf{V}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}]
in
V
,

(142)

\displaystyle|Z_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}-Z_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}}|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i\in[n]}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\mathbf{V}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\right|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

(143)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i\in[n]}\left|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\mathbf{V}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\right|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

(144)

\displaystyle\leq 2C\sum_{i\in[n]}\left|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\prime})\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\right|^{2}_{\psi_{2}}+2C\sum_{i\in[n]}\left|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\prime}(\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime})\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\right|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}

(145)

\displaystyle\leq 2CL^{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}^{2}+2CL^{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|_{\Pi}^{2}\leq 2CL^{2}\mathsf{d}([\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}],[\mathbf{V}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}]),

with the pseudo-metric
\mathsf{d}([\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}],[\mathbf{V}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}]):=\sqrt{\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|_{\Pi}^{2}}
, using that

\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\leq 1
and
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq 1
. On the other hand, by definition of the process
W
it is easy to check that

(146)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(W_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}-W_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}})^{2}]=L^{2}(\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|_{\Pi}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}).

From (
145
),(
146
), we conclude that the processes
W
and
Z
satisfy the conditions of Talagrand’s majoration and minoration generic chaining bounds for sub-Gaussian processes (e.g. Theorems 8.5.5 and 8.6.1 in
[
1
]
). Hence, there is a universal numerical constant
C>0
such that, for any
t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t^{2}}
,

(147)

\displaystyle\sup_{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in V}|Z_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}|\leq CL\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in V}W_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}\right]+t\right\}.

In above we used that
Z_{\mathbf{V}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}=0
at
[\mathbf{V}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}]=0
and that the diameter of
V\subset\mathbb{B}_{\Pi}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}\times\mathbb{B}_{2}^{n}
under the metric

\mathsf{d}
is less than
2\sqrt{2}
. We also have

(148)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in V}W_{\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}}\bigg]\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{\mathbf{V}\in V_{1}}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\Xi},\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbf{V})\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\bigg]+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in V_{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\xi}\rangle\bigg]=\mathscr{G}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(V_{1}))+\mathscr{G}(V_{2}).

Joining the two previous inequalities complete the proof of the claimed inequality.
∎

Proposition 4

(
\IP
)
.

There exists universal constant
C>0
, such that for all
\delta\in(0,1]

and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with probability at least

1-\delta
, the following property holds: for all

[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(149)

\displaystyle\left|\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq CL\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}}\big\|\mathbf{V}\big\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}

(150)

\displaystyle\quad+CL\frac{\mathscr{G}\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\cap\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\mathbb{B}_{F}\right)}{\sqrt{n}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}+CL\frac{\mathscr{G}\left(\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u})\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{n}\cap\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\mathbb{B}_{2}^{n}\right)}{\sqrt{n}}\big\|\mathbf{V}\big\|_{\Pi}.

Proof.

Let
R_{1},R_{2}>0
and define the sets

(151)

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle:=\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\leq 1,\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\leq R_{1}\},

(152)

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle:=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}=1,\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u})\leq R_{2}\}.

Note that,

(153)

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(V_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq R_{1}\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\cap R_{1}^{-1}\mathbb{B}_{F}\right),\quad\quad\mathscr{G}(V_{2})\leq R_{2}\mathscr{G}\left(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{n}\cap R_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{B}_{2}^{n}\right).

Define for convenience the functions

(154)

\displaystyle g(r):=CL\frac{\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\cap r^{-1}\mathbb{B}_{F}\right)}{\sqrt{n}}r,\quad\bar{g}(\bar{r}):=CL\frac{\mathscr{G}\left(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{n}\cap\bar{r}^{-1}\mathbb{B}_{2}^{n}\right)}{\sqrt{n}}\bar{r}.

By Lemma
8.12
, there is universal constant
C>0
such that, for any
R_{1},R_{2}>0
and
\delta\in(0,1]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the following inequality holds:

(155)

\displaystyle\sup_{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in V_{1}\times V_{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\rangle\leq g(R_{1})+\bar{g}(R_{2})+CL\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}.

We use the above property and the bi-parameter peeling Lemma
11.2
with constraint set
V:=\mathbb{B}_{\Pi}\times\mathbb{B}_{2}^{n}
, functions

M(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}):=-\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\rangle
,

h(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}):=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})
,
\bar{h}(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}):=\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u})
,
g
and
\bar{g}
and constants
c:=1
and
b:=CL\sqrt{1/n}
. Note that the claimed inequality trivially holds if
\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}=0
or
\boldsymbol{u}=0
. Indeed, since
\mathbf{X}_{i}
is
L
-sub-Gaussian,
\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}=0
implies that
\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=0
with probability 1. The desired inequality follows from Lemma
11.2
combined with the fact that

[\nicefrac{{\mathbf{V}}}{{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}}};\nicefrac{{\boldsymbol{u}}}{{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}}}]\in V,

for all
[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\neq 0
and
\boldsymbol{u}\neq 0
and the homogeneity of norms.
∎

We now turn our attention to property
\MP
in Definition
8.1
. Of course,

\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\rangle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\xi_{i}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{X}_{i}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\xi_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}.

The control of the first term will follow from a bound for the multiplier process (Theorem
11.6
in the supplemental material). As for the second, one may avoid chaining by using a standard symmetrization-contraction argument which we present for completeness.

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
U
be any bounded subset of
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{2}
.

Then, for any
n\geq 1
and
t>0
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-t^{2}/2)
,
we have

\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in U}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{u}\rangle\leq 8\sigma\left[\mathscr{G}\big(V_{2}\big)+t\right].

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector whose components are iid Rademacher random variables. Let
t\geq 0
. The symmetrization inequality (e.g. Exercise 11.5 in
[
5
]
) yields

(156)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(t\sup_{\boldsymbol{z}\in U}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{z}\rangle\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(t\sup_{\boldsymbol{z}\in U}2\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\odot\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{z}\rangle\right)\right],

where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\odot\boldsymbol{\xi}
is the vector with components

\{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. For each
i\in[n]
,
\epsilon_{i}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
is a symmetric sub-Gaussian random variable with
\psi_{2}
-norm not greater than
\sigma
. Let
\boldsymbol{g}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{n})
a standard normal vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Hence, the following tail dominance holds:

\mathbb{P}(|\epsilon_{i}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}|>\tau)\leq 4\mathbb{P}(\sigma|\boldsymbol{g}_{i}|>\tau)
for all
i\in[n]
and for all
\tau>0
. From the contraction principle as stated in Lemma 4.6 in
[
9
]
,

(157)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(t\sup_{\boldsymbol{z}\in U}2\langle\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\odot\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{z}\rangle\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(8\sigma t\sup_{\boldsymbol{z}\in U}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{z}\rangle\right)\right].

Since
U\subset\mathbb{B}_{2}^{n}
, the function
\boldsymbol{g}\mapsto\sup_{\boldsymbol{z}\in U}\langle\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{z}\rangle
is
1
-Lipschitz under the
\ell_{2}
-norm. By Theorem 5.5 in
[
5
]
, the RHS of the previous inequality is upper bounded by

(158)

\displaystyle\exp\left(8\sigma t\mathscr{G}(U)+32t^{2}\sigma^{2}\right).

A standard Chernoff bound concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5

(
\MP
)
.

For
t,s>0
define

\triangle(t,s):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\log t}+\frac{1}{n}\log t+\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{(\log t)(\log s)}+\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{\log s}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}[1+\sqrt{\log s}].

Then there exists universal constants
C>0
,
c_{0},c\geq 2
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\delta\in(0,1/c]
and
\rho\in(0,1/c_{0}]
, with probability at least

1-\delta-\rho
, the following property holds: for all

[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(159)

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma L\cdot\triangle(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\delta}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho}})\cdot\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}

(160)

\displaystyle+C\sigma L\left[1+\frac{\sqrt{\log(1/\rho)}}{\sqrt{n}}\right]\frac{\mathscr{G}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}}))}{\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})+C\sigma\frac{\mathscr{G}\big(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}\big)}{\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}).

Proof.

Define the set

(161)

\displaystyle V_{0}:=\{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]:\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}\leq 1,\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\leq R_{1},\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u})\leq R_{2}\}.

Theorem
11.6
in the Appendix (together with Talagrand’s majorization theorem), Lemma
8.13
and an union bound imply that, for universal constants
C>0
and
c,c_{0}\geq 2
, for all
\delta\in(0,1/c)
and
\rho\in(0,1/c_{0})
, with probability at least
1-\delta-\rho
,

(162)

\displaystyle\sup_{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\in V_{0}}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma L\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\rho)}{n}}+1\right)\frac{\mathscr{G}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\big)}{\sqrt{n}}R_{1}

(163)

\displaystyle+C\sigma L\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)\log(1/\rho)}{n^{2}}}\right)

(164)

\displaystyle+C\sigma\frac{\mathscr{G}\big(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{2}\big)}{\sqrt{n}}R_{2}+C\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}.

We will now apply Lemma
11.3
with set
V:=\{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]:\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}\leq 1\}
, functions
M(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}):=-\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u})\rangle
,
h(\mathbf{V}):=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})
,
\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u})
,

g(R_{1}):=\mathscr{G}\big(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\big)R_{1},

\bar{g}(R_{2}):=\mathscr{G}\big(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{2}\big)R_{2}/L,

and constant
b:=C\sigma L
, where we recall
L\geq 1
. The result then follow from such lemma and homogeneity noting that

\frac{[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]}{\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}}\in V_{0}
for
[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]
such that
\|[\mathbf{V};\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}\neq 0
.
∎

8.4
Proof of Theorem
4.1

We now set
\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{1}
,

\mathcal{Q}:=\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
. A standard Gaussian maximal inequality implies
\mathscr{G}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbb{B}_{1}^{p})\lesssim\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\log p}
. Proposition E.2 in
[
7
]
implies

\mathscr{G}(\mathbb{B}_{\sharp})\lesssim 1
.

We next use Proposition
3
with
\epsilon=\frac{c}{C^{2}L^{2}}
for sufficiently small
c\in(0,1)
and assuming

(165)

\displaystyle\delta\geq\exp\left(-c_{1}\frac{n}{L^{4}}\right),

for large enough universal constant
c_{1}>0
. It follows that for an universal constant
\mathsf{a}_{1}\in(0,1)
and

\mathsf{a}_{2}\asymp L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},

on an event
\Omega_{1}
of probability at least
1-\delta/3
, property

\TP_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(\mathsf{a}_{1};\mathsf{a}_{2})
is satisfied.

From Proposition
4
, for every
\delta\in(0,1)
and

\mathsf{b}_{1}\asymp L\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}},\quad\mathsf{b}_{2}\asymp L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},\quad\mathsf{b}_{3}\asymp\frac{L}{\sqrt{n}},

on an event
\Omega_{2}
of probability at least

, property

is satisfied.

From Lemma
8.2
, by enlarging
c_{1}
if necessary, for an universal constant
\mathsf{c}_{1}\in(0,1)
and

\mathsf{c}_{2}\asymp L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},\quad\mathsf{c}_{3}\asymp\frac{L}{\sqrt{n}},

is satisfied on
\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}
.

We now use Proposition

(with
\delta=\rho
). By enlarging
c_{1}
in (

) if necessary, if we take

\mathsf{f}_{1}\asymp\sigma L\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}},\quad\mathsf{f}_{2}\asymp\sigma L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},\quad\mathsf{f}_{3}\asymp\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},

we have by Proposition
5
that on an event

\Omega_{3}
of probability at least
1-\delta/3
,

\MP_{\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2};\mathsf{f}_{3})
is satisfied.

By an union bound and enlarging constants, for
\delta
satisfying (
165
), on the event

\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}\cap\Omega_{3}
of probability at least
1-\delta
, all properties
\TP
,
\IP
,
\ATP
and

\MP
hold with constants as specified above. We next assume such event is realized and invoke Theorem
8.10
. It is straightforward to check

\rm{(iii)}
by the definitions of
\tau
and
\lambda=\gamma\tau
in Theorem
4.1
. Note that that
R\leq\sqrt{s}\mu(\boldsymbol{b}^{*})
with
\mu(\boldsymbol{b}^{*}):=\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*},\|\cdot\|_{1}}(12))
. In item

, conditions (

) and (

) require checking

\displaystyle C\sigma^{2}L^{2}\rho_{1}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{b}^{*})\frac{s\log p}{n}+C\sigma^{2}L^{2}\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)<1/2,

and that
\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}<1/2
. Assuming further that
\delta\geq\exp\left(-c_{2}\frac{n}{\sigma^{2}L^{2}}\right)
for universal constant

and enlarging

above, item

\rm{(iv)}
is satisfied. With all conditions of Theorem

taking place, the rate in Theorem

follows.

Note that

and

are bounded by a numerical constant of

.

Remark 5
.

With some abuse of notation, let
\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
denote the Slope norm in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with the sequence
\bar{w}_{j}=\log(\bar{A}p/j)
for some
\bar{A}\geq 2
. The proof with
\mathcal{R}=\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
follows a similar path. One difference is that we need to consider different cones. For
c_{0},\gamma>0
, we define

(167)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(c_{0})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\sum_{i=s+1}^{p}\bar{\omega}_{j}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\sharp}\leq c_{0}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}^{2}}\right\},

(168)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(c_{0},\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\left\{[\boldsymbol{v};\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\gamma\sum_{i=s+1}^{p}\bar{\omega}_{j}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\sharp}+\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}\leq c_{0}\left[\gamma\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\right]\right\}.

In this setting,
\mu(\boldsymbol{b}^{*}):=\mu(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(12))
. We also use the bound

\mathscr{G}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbb{B}_{\sharp})\lesssim\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
, which follows from Proposition E.2 in
[
7
]
. We omit the details.

8.5
Proof of Theorem
4.2

The proof follows exact same guidelines as the Proof of Theorem
4.1
. The changes are that for the nuclear norm
\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{N}
, we have

\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{N}}))\lesssim\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})(\sqrt{d_{1}}+\sqrt{d_{2}})
by Lemma H.1 in
[
68
]

and
R\leq\sqrt{r}\mu(\mathbf{B}^{*})
with
\mu(\mathbf{B}^{*}):=\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*},\|\cdot\|_{N}}(12))
.

9
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Recall estimator (
2.2
).

Throughout Sections
9.1

and

9.2
, we see

\|\cdot\|_{\Pi}
as a generic pseudo-norm and

\{(\mathbf{X}_{i},\xi_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
as a deterministic sequence satisfying (
10
) where

\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{i})_{i\in[n]}
and
\mathfrak{X}
is the design operator associated to the sequence
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Probabilistic assumptions are used only in Sections
9.3
and

9.4
.

9.1
Additional design properties and cones

In the following, let
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be norms on

\mathds{R}^{p}
. We first present a definition bounding the
product process
:

(169)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{V};\mathbf{W}]\mapsto\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigg[\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle-\mathbb{E}[\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle]\bigg].

For convenience, we define the empirical bilinear form

\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{V},(\nicefrac{{\mathfrak{X}^{*}\circ\mathfrak{X}}}{{n}})(\mathbf{W})\rangle,

where
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
denotes the adjunct operator of
\mathfrak{X}
.

Definition 9.1

(
\PP
)
.

Given positive numbers
(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4})
, we say that
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies

\PP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1};\mathsf{b}_{2};\mathsf{b}_{3};\mathsf{b}_{4})
if for all
[\mathbf{V};\mathbf{W}]\in\mathds{R}^{p}
,

(170)

\displaystyle\left|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{n}-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{b}_{1}\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\Pi}\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{b}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{b}_{3}\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\Pi}\mathcal{Q}(\mathbf{W})

(171)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{b}_{4}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\mathcal{Q}(\mathbf{W}).

We will also need variations of
\ATP
and
\MP
in Definition
8.1
which will be used throughout Section
9
.

Definition 9.2

(
\ATP
and
\MP
)
.
