\mu_{k}:=\mu\eta^{k-1}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we define
8
8

8

Here
g^{-1}
is the generalized inverse
defined by
g^{-1}(x)=\inf\{a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:g(a)\geq x\}
.

\nu_{k}:=g^{-1}(\mu_{k})
and the set

V_{k}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in V:\mu_{k}\leq(g\circ h)(\boldsymbol{v})<\mu_{k+1}\}.

The union bound and the fact that
\sum_{k\geq 1}k^{-1-\epsilon}\leq 1+\epsilon^{-1}
imply that the event

A:=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}A\left(\nu_{k},0,\frac{\epsilon\delta}{(1+\epsilon)k^{1+\epsilon}},\frac{\epsilon\rho}{(1+\epsilon)k^{1+\epsilon}}\right),

has a probability at least
1-c\delta-c_{0}\rho
. For convenience, we define
\triangle(t):=\log\{(1+\epsilon)/(\epsilon t)\}
and
\triangle_{k}:=({1+\epsilon})\log k
. Throughout the proof, assume that this event is realized:

(313)

\displaystyle\forall k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\quad\begin{cases}\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in V\text{ such that }h(\boldsymbol{v})\leq\nu_{k}\text{ we have }\\
M(\boldsymbol{v})\geq-[1+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{k}}]b\frac{g(\nu_{k})}{\sqrt{n}}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{k}}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})[\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{k}]\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\sqrt{[\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{k}][\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{k}]}.\end{cases}

For every
\boldsymbol{v}\in V
, there is
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that

\boldsymbol{v}\in V_{\ell}
. If
\ell\geq 1
, then
h(\boldsymbol{v})\leq\nu_{\ell+1}
. This fact and (
313
) implies

(314)

\displaystyle M(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\geq-[1+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{\ell}}]b(\nicefrac{{\mu}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\eta^{\ell}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell}}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})[\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{\ell}]

(315)

\displaystyle-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\sqrt{[\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell}][\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{\ell}]}

(316)

\displaystyle\geq-[1+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\rho)}]b(\nicefrac{{\eta^{2}}}{{\sqrt{n}}})(g\circ h)(\boldsymbol{v})-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\triangle(\rho)-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)\triangle(\rho)}-\lozenge_{\ell},

where

(317)

\displaystyle\lozenge_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=[1+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{\ell}}](b\nicefrac{{\mu}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\eta^{\ell}+(\nicefrac{{b}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell}}+(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})[\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{\ell}]

(318)

\displaystyle+(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\sqrt{[\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell}][\triangle(\rho)+\triangle_{\ell}]}

(319)

\displaystyle-[1+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\rho)}](\nicefrac{{b\mu}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\eta^{\ell+1}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\triangle(\rho)-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)\triangle(\rho)}.

By appropriately choosing
\mu>0
and
\epsilon,\eta>1
, a standard calculation shows that
\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\lozenge_{\ell}\leq C\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}[1+\sqrt{\triangle(\rho)}]
.

If
\ell=0
, then (
313
) with
k=1
and using
g(\nu_{1})=\mu
lead to

(320)

\displaystyle M(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\geq-[1+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\rho)}](\nicefrac{{b\mu}}{{\sqrt{n}}})-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\triangle(\rho)-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)\triangle(\rho)}.

Joining the two lower bounds establish the claim.
∎

Lemma 11.4
.

Let
h,\bar{h}
be functions from
V
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
b>0
and
\omega\geq 1
be constants and
c\geq 1
be a universal constant. Assume that for every
r,\bar{r}>0
and every
\delta\in(0,1/c)
, the event
A(r,\bar{r},\delta)
defined by the inequality

(321)

\displaystyle\inf_{\boldsymbol{v}\in V:(h,\bar{h})(\boldsymbol{v})\leq(r,\bar{r})}M(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\geq-b\frac{\omega}{n}r\bar{r}-b\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\cdot\bar{r}-\frac{b}{n}\log(1/\delta),

has probability at least
1-c\delta
.

Then, for universal constant
C>0
, with probability at least
1-c\delta
, we have that for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in V
,

(322)

\displaystyle M(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\geq-Cb\frac{\omega}{n}[1+h(\boldsymbol{v})\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{v})]-C\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\cdot\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{v})-C\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}[1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}]-C\frac{b}{n}\log(1/\delta).

Proof.

The proof is an adaptation of proof of Lemmas
11.2
and
11.3
. One notable change is that the peeled functions
g(r)=r
and
\bar{g}(\bar{r})=\bar{r}
are multiplied. We only give a sketch of the proof. In the following,
C
is an universal constant that may change.

Let
\eta,\epsilon>1
be two parameters to be chosen later on. Let
\mu_{0}:=0
and
\mu_{k}:=\mu\eta^{k-1}
for
k\geq 1
. For
k,\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we define the set

V_{k,\bar{k}}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{v}\in V:\mu_{k}\leq h(\boldsymbol{v})<\mu_{k+1},\quad\mu_{\bar{k}}\leq\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{v})<\mu_{\bar{k}+1}\right\}.

The union bound and the fact that
\sum_{k,\bar{k}\geq 1}(k\bar{k})^{-1-\epsilon}\leq(1+\epsilon^{-1})^{2}
imply that the event

A=\bigcap_{k,\bar{k}\geq 1}^{\infty}A\Big(\mu_{k},\bar{\mu}_{\bar{k}},\frac{\epsilon^{2}\delta}{(1+\epsilon)^{2}(k\bar{k})^{1+\epsilon}}\Big)

has probability at least
1-c\delta
. To ease notation, set

\triangle(\delta):=\log\{(1+\epsilon)^{2}/(\epsilon^{2}\delta)\}
and
\triangle_{k,\bar{k}}:=({1+\epsilon})\log(k\bar{k})
. We assume in the sequel that the event
A
is realized, that is

(323)

\displaystyle\forall k,\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\quad\begin{cases}\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in V\text{ such that }(h,\bar{h})(\boldsymbol{v})\leq(\mu_{k},\mu_{\bar{k}})\text{ we have }\\
M(\boldsymbol{v})\geq-b\frac{\omega}{n}\mu_{k}\mu_{\bar{k}}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{k,\bar{k}}}\cdot\mu_{\bar{k}}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})[\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{k,\bar{k}}].\end{cases}

For every
\boldsymbol{v}\in V
, there are
\ell,\bar{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that

\boldsymbol{v}\in V_{\ell,\bar{\ell}}
. If
\ell\geq 1
or
\bar{\ell}\geq 1
, then
h(\boldsymbol{v})\leq\mu_{\ell+1}
and
\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{v})\leq\mu_{\bar{\ell}+1}
. This fact and (
323
) imply

(324)

\displaystyle M(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\geq-b\frac{\omega}{n}\mu^{2}\eta^{\ell}\eta^{\bar{\ell}}-\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell+1,\bar{\ell}+1}}\cdot\mu\eta^{\bar{\ell}}-\frac{b}{n}[\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell+1,\bar{\ell}+1}]

(325)

\displaystyle\geq-b\frac{\omega}{n}\eta^{4}h(\boldsymbol{v})\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{v})-\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)}\cdot\eta^{2}\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{v})-\frac{b}{n}\triangle(\delta)-\lozenge_{\ell,\bar{\ell}}

where

(326)

\displaystyle\lozenge_{\ell,\bar{\ell}}

\displaystyle:=b\frac{\omega}{n}\mu^{2}\eta^{\ell}\eta^{\bar{\ell}}+\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell+1,\bar{\ell}+1}}\cdot\mu\eta^{\bar{\ell}}+\frac{b}{n}[\triangle(\delta)+\triangle_{\ell+1,\bar{\ell}+1}]

(327)

\displaystyle-b\frac{\omega}{n}\mu^{2}\eta^{k+1}\eta^{\bar{\ell}+1}-\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)}\mu\eta^{\bar{\ell}+1}-\frac{b}{n}\triangle(\delta).

By appropriately choosing
\mu,\eta>1
, one shows that

\sup_{\ell,\bar{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\lozenge_{\ell,\bar{\ell}}\leq C\frac{b}{\sqrt{n}}[1+\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)}]
for a universal constant
C>0
.

If
\ell=\bar{\ell}=0
, then (
323
) with
k=\bar{k}=1
leads to

(328)

\displaystyle M(\boldsymbol{v})\geq-b\frac{\omega}{n}\mu^{2}-(\nicefrac{{b\mu}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\triangle(\delta)}-(\nicefrac{{b}}{{n}})\triangle(\delta).

Joining the two lower bounds establish the claim.
∎

11.2
Multiplier process

Throughout this section,
(B,\mathcal{B},\mathbf{P})
is a probability space,
(\xi,X)
is a random (possibly not independent) pair taking values
on
\mathbb{R}\times B
and
X
has marginal distribution
\mathbf{P}
.
\{(\xi_{i},X_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
will denote an iid copy of
(\xi,X)
and
\hat{\mathbf{P}}
be denotes the empirical measure associated to
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

We are interested in concentration inequalities for the
multiplier process

[
65
]

M(f):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}(\xi_{i}f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\xi f(X)]),

defined over a subgaussian class of measurable functions
f:B\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Let
L_{\psi_{2}}=L_{\psi_{2}}(\mathbf{P})
be the family of measurable functions
f:B\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
having finite
\psi_{2}
-norm

\|f\|_{\psi_{2}}:=|f(X)|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\{c>0:\mathbb{E}[\psi_{2}(\nicefrac{{f(X)}}{{c}})]\leq 1\}

where
\psi_{2}(t):=e^{t^{2}}-1
. We will also assume that the
\psi_{2}
-norm of
\xi
, denoted also by

\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}
, is finite. Given
f,g\in L_{\psi_{2}}
, we set

\mathsf{d}_{\psi_{2}}(f,g):=\|f-g\|_{\psi_{2}},

\langle f,g\rangle_{n}:=\hat{\mathbf{P}}fg

and

\|f\|_{n}:=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{n}}.

We recall the Hölder-type inequality
\|fg\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\|f\|_{\psi_{2}}\|g\|_{\psi_{2}}
.

One pioneering idea is of “generic chaining” developed by Talagrand
[
81
]
and recently refined by Dirksen, Bednorz, Mendelson and collaborators
[
2
,
36
,
5
,
65
]
. The following notion of complexity is used in generic chaining bounds.

Definition 11.5

(
\gamma_{2,p}
-functional)
.

Let
(T,\mathsf{d})
be a pseudo-metric space. We say a sequence
(T_{k})
of subsets of
T
is
admissible
if
|T_{0}|=1
and
|T_{k}|\leq 2^{2^{k}}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\cup_{k\geq 0}T_{k}
is dense in
T
. Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the class of all such admissible subset sequences. Given
p\geq 1
, the

\gamma_{2,p}
-functional with respect to
(T,\mathsf{d})
is the quantity

(329)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2,p}(T,\mathsf{d}):=\inf_{(T_{k})\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{t\in T}\sum_{k\geq\lfloor\log_{2}p\rfloor}2^{k/2}\mathsf{d}(t,T_{k}).

We will say that
(T_{k})\in\mathcal{A}
is optimal if it achieves the infimum above. Set
\gamma_{2}(T,\mathsf{d}):=\gamma_{2,1}(T,\mathsf{d})
.

For the rest of this section, we set
\mathsf{d}:=\mathsf{d}_{\psi_{2}}
and omit the subscript
\mathsf{d}
for convenience. Given a subclass
F\subset L_{\psi_{2}}
, we let
\Delta(F):=\sup_{f,f^{\prime}\in F}\mathsf{d}(f,f^{\prime})
and
\bar{\Delta}(F):=\sup_{f\in F}\mathsf{d}(f,0)
.

We will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 11.6

(Multiplier process)
.

There exist universal constants
c>0
, such that for all
f_{0}\in F
,
n\geq 1
,
u\geq 1
and
v\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-ce^{-u/4}-ce^{-nv}
,

(330)

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in F}|M(f)-M(f_{0})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sqrt{v}+1\right)\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}+\left(\sqrt{\frac{2u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{uv}{n}}\right)\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\bar{\Delta}(F)

Remark 6
.

Mendelson
[
65
]
established impressive concentration inequalities for the multiplier process. In fact, they hold for much more general
(\xi,X)
having heavier tails (see Theorems 1.9 and 4.4 in
[
65
]
). When specifying these bounds to subgaussian classes, however, the confidence parameter
u>0
multiplies the complexity functional
\gamma_{2}(F)
. In Theorem
11.6
,
u>0
does not multiply
\gamma_{2}(F)
, a fact that will be useful for our purposes mentioned in item (c) of Section
1
.

Our proof is inspired by Dirksen’s method
[
36
]
which obtained concentration inequalities for the
quadratic process
(Theorem
8.11
in Section
8.3
). One key observation used by Dirksen
[
36
]
and Bednorz
[
5
]
is that one must bound the chain differently for
k\leq\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor
, the so called “subgaussian path” and
k\geq\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor
, the “subexponential path”. In bounding the multiplier process, we additionally introduce a “lazy walked” chain, a technique already present in Talagrand’s original bound for the
empirical process

[
81
]
. The following lemma is proved similarly to Lemma 5.4 in
[
36
]
so we omit the proof.

Lemma 11.7
.

Let
f,f^{\prime}\in L_{\psi_{2}}
.

If for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
2^{k/2}\leq\sqrt{n}
, then for any
u>0
, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-(2^{k}+u))
,

(331)

\displaystyle|M(f)-M(f^{\prime})|\leq\left[(1+\sqrt{2})\frac{2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{2u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}\right]\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\psi_{2}}.

If for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
2^{k/2}\geq\sqrt{n}
, then for any
u\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-(2^{k}+u))
,

(332)

\displaystyle\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{n}\leq(\sqrt{u}+2^{k/2})\frac{[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(f,f^{\prime}).

Proof of Theorem
11.6
.

Let
(F_{k})
be an optimal admissible sequences for

\gamma_{2}(F)
. Let
(\mathcal{F}_{k})
be defined by
\mathcal{F}_{0}:=F_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{k}:=\cup_{j\leq k}F_{j}
so that
|\mathcal{F}_{k}|\leq 2|F_{k}|=2^{2^{k}+1}
. Set
k_{0}:=\min\{k\geq 1:2^{k/2}>\sqrt{n}\}
and let us define

\mathcal{I}:=\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\ell<k<k_{0}\}

and

\mathcal{J}:=\{k\in\mathbb{N}:k\geq k_{0}\}
.
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in F
, let

\Pi_{k}(f)\in\argmin_{f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mathsf{d}(f,f^{\prime}),

Given
f\in F
, we take some
\Pi_{0}(f)\in F
and for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the “lazy walk” chain selection by:

(333)

\displaystyle k_{j}(f):=\inf\left\{j\geq k_{j-1}(f):\mathsf{d}(f,\Pi_{j}(f))\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}(f,\Pi_{k_{j-1}(f)}(f))\right\}.

For simplicity of notation, we will set
\pi_{j}(f):=\Pi_{k_{j}(f)}(f)
. For
f\in F
, our proof will rely on the chain:

(334)

\displaystyle M(f)-M(\pi_{0}(f))

\displaystyle=\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{J}}\left[M(\pi_{j+1}(f))-M(\pi_{j}(f))\right]+\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{I}}\left[M(\pi_{j}(f))-M(\pi_{j-1}(f))\right],

where we have used that
\cup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_{k}
is dense on
F
.

Fix
u\geq 1
. Given any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define the event

\Omega_{k,\mathcal{I},u}
for which, for all
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, we have

(335)

\displaystyle|M(f)-M(f^{\prime})|\leq\left[(1+\sqrt{2})\frac{2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{2u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}\right]\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\psi_{2}}.

Define also the event
\Omega_{k,\mathcal{J},u}
for which, for all
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, we have

(336)

\displaystyle\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{n}

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{u}+2^{k/2})\frac{[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\psi_{2}}.

For simplicity, define the vector

\boldsymbol{\xi}:=(\xi_{i})_{i\in[n]}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}
. Given
v\geq 1
, we define the event

\Omega_{\xi,v}
, for which

(337)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{n}\leq[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\sqrt{v}.

By an union bound over all possible pairs

(\pi_{k-1}(f),\pi_{k}(f))
we have
|\Omega_{k,\mathcal{I},u}|\leq|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}||\mathcal{F}_{k}|\leq 2^{2^{k+1}}
. If

\Omega_{\mathcal{I},u}:=\cap_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\Omega_{k,\mathcal{I},u}
, the first bound on Lemma
11.7
for
k\in\mathcal{I}
and a standard union bound using the geometric series
9
9

9

See e.g. a modification of Lemma A.4 in
[
36
]
.
imply that there is universal constant
c>0

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{I},u}^{c})\leq ce^{-u/4}.

Similarly, the second bound in Lemma
11.7
for
k\in\mathcal{J}
imply that for the event

\Omega_{\mathcal{J},u}:=\cap_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\Omega_{k,\mathcal{J},u}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{J},u}^{c})\leq ce^{-u/4}.

Using Bernstein’s inequality for
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
we get
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\xi,v}^{c})\leq ce^{-vn}
. Hence, the event

\Omega_{u,v}:=\Omega_{\mathcal{I},u}\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{J},u}\cap\Omega_{\xi,v}
has

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{u,v}^{c})\leq ce^{-u/4}+ce^{-vn}
.

We next fix
u\geq 2
and
v\geq 1
and assume that
\Omega_{u,v}
always holds. We now bound the chain over
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
separately.

The subgaussian path
\mathcal{I}
.
Given
j
such that
k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{I}
, since
\pi_{j}(f),\pi_{j-1}(f)\in\mathcal{F}_{k_{j}(f)}
, we may apply (
335
) to
k:=k_{j}(f)
so that

(338)

\displaystyle|M(\pi_{j}(f))-M(\pi_{j-1}(f))|\leq\left[(1+\sqrt{2})\frac{2^{k_{j}(f)/2}}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{2u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}\right]\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\|\pi_{j}(f)-\pi_{j-1}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}.

We note that, by triangle inequality and minimality of
k_{j-1}(f)
,

(339)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{j}(f)-\pi_{j-1}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\mathsf{d}(f,\mathcal{F}_{k_{j}(f)})+\mathsf{d}(f,\mathcal{F}_{k_{j-1}(f)})\leq\mathsf{d}(f,\mathcal{F}_{k_{j}(f)})+2\mathsf{d}(f,\mathcal{F}_{k_{j}(f)-1}),

so that

(340)

\displaystyle\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{I}}2^{k_{j}(f)/2}\|\pi_{j}(f)-\pi_{j-1}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq(1+2\sqrt{2})\gamma_{2}(F).

Moreover, by the definition of the lazy walked chain and a geometric series bound,

(341)

\displaystyle\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{I}}\|\pi_{j}(f)-\pi_{j-1}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq 4\mathsf{d}(f,\mathcal{F}_{0})\leq 4\bar{\Delta}(F).

We thus conclude that

(342)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{I}}[M(\pi_{j}(f))-M(\pi_{j-1}(f))]\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\sqrt{\frac{2u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}\right)\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\bar{\Delta}(F)+(1+\sqrt{2})(1+2\sqrt{2})\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}.

The subexponential path
\mathcal{J}
.
Let us denote by
\mathbf{Q}
the joint distribution of
(\xi,X)
and
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
the empirical distribution associated to
\{(\xi_{i},X_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
. In particular,
M(f)=\hat{\mathbf{Q}}(\cdot)f-\mathbf{Q}\hat{\mathbf{Q}}(\cdot)f
. By Jensen’s and triangle inequalities,

(343)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{J}}[M(\pi_{j+1}(f))-M(\pi_{j}(f))]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{J}}\hat{\mathbf{Q}}(\cdot)\left|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\right|

(344)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbf{Q}\hat{\mathbf{Q}}(\cdot)\left|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\right|.

For convenience, we set
\hat{T}_{j}:=\hat{\mathbf{Q}}(\cdot)\left|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\right|
.

Given
j
such that
k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{J}
, since

\pi_{j+1}(f),\pi_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{F}_{k_{j+1}(f)}
, we may apply (
336
) to
k:=k_{j+1}(f)
. This fact, (
337
) and Cauchy-Schwarz yield

(345)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{n}\|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\|_{n}\leq\sqrt{v}[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}(\sqrt{u}+2^{k_{j+1}(f)/2})\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}.

In a similar fashion, we can also state that with probability at least
1-ce^{-u/4}
,

(346)

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{T}_{j}}{\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{n}}\leq(\sqrt{u}+2^{k_{j+1}(f)/2})\frac{[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}},

so integrating the tail leads to

(347)

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\hat{T}_{j}}{\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{n}}\right)^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\leq c2^{k_{j+1}(f)/2}\frac{[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}},

and by Hölder’s inequality,

(348)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\hat{T}_{j}\leq\left\{\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{n}^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\hat{T}_{j}}{\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{n}}\right)^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\leq c[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}\frac{\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{2^{k_{j+1}(f)/2}\|\pi_{j+1}(f)-\pi_{j}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}.

We thus conclude from (
344
) and a analogous reasoning to (
340
) and (
341
) that

(349)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j:k_{j}(f)\in\mathcal{J}}[M(\pi_{j+1}(f))-M(\pi_{j}(f))]\right|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}^{2}\sqrt{v}\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\sqrt{u}\frac{4\bar{\Delta}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}

(350)

\displaystyle+\left(c_{1}^{2}\sqrt{v}+c_{1}\frac{c}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}(1+2\sqrt{2})\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)}{\sqrt{n}},

with
c_{1}:=[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}
.

From the above bound, (
342
) and (
334
) we conclude that, for any
u\geq 2
and
v\geq 1
, on the event
\Omega_{u,v}
of probability at least
1-ce^{-u/4}-ce^{-nv}
, we have the bound stated in the theorem.
∎

11.3
Product process

With the same setting and definitions of Section
11.2
, we are now interested in concentration bounds for the
product process

[
65
]

A(f,g):=\hat{\mathbf{P}}(fg-\mathbf{P}fg)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigg\{f(X_{i})g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}f(X_{i})g(X_{i})\bigg\},

defined over two distinct subgaussian classes
F
and
G
of measurable functions. We will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 11.8

(Product process)
.

Let
F,G
be subclasses of
L_{\psi_{2}}
. For any
1\leq p<\infty
,

(351)

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.72218pt\,\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}|A(f,g)|\,\kern-1.72218pt\right|}_{p}\lesssim\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{n}+\bar{\Delta}(F)\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{\sqrt{n}}+\bar{\Delta}(G)\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}+\bar{\Delta}(F)\bar{\Delta}(G)\left(\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}+\frac{p}{n}\right).

In particular, there exist universal constants
c,C>0
, such that for all
n\geq 1
and
u\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-e^{-u}
,

(352)

\displaystyle\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}\left|A(f,g)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left[\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)\gamma_{2}(G)}{n}+\bar{\Delta}(F)\frac{\gamma_{2}(G)}{\sqrt{n}}+\bar{\Delta}(G)\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}\right]

(353)

\displaystyle+c\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}\|fg-\mathbf{P}fg\|_{\psi_{1}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}\right).

Remark 7
.

Again, Mendelson
[
65
]
proved concentration inequalities for the product process for much more general classes having heavier tails (see Theorem 1.13 in
[
65
]
). When specifying these bounds for subgaussian classes, the confidence parameter
u>0
multiplies the complexity functionals. The fact that in Theorem
11.8
the confidence parameter
u>0
does not multiply the complexity functionals

\gamma_{2}(F)
and
\gamma_{2}(G)
will be useful for the purpose specified in item (c) in Section
1
.

Before proving the theorem we will need some auxiliary results. The following lemma is proved similarly to Lemma 5.4 in
[
36
]
so we omit the proof.

Lemma 11.9
.

Let
f,f^{\prime}
and
g,g^{\prime}
in
L_{\psi_{2}}
.

If for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
2^{k/2}\leq\sqrt{n}
, then for any
u\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-2^{k}u)
,

(354)

\displaystyle|A(f,g)-A(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})|\leq 2(1+\sqrt{2})\frac{u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\|fg-f^{\prime}g^{\prime}\|_{\psi_{1}}.

If for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
2^{k/2}\geq\sqrt{n}
, then for any
u\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-2^{k}u)
,

(355)

\displaystyle\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{n}\leq\sqrt{u}2^{k/2}\frac{[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(f,f^{\prime}).

As in the proof of Theorem
11.6
, we combine Dirksen’s method
[
36
]
with Talagrand’s
[
81
]
“lazy-walked” chain. One difference now is that we will explicitly need Dirksen’s bound for the
quadratic process

A(f):=\hat{\mathbf{P}}(f^{2}-\mathbf{P}f^{2}).

The following proposition is a corollary of the proof of Theorem 5.5 in
[
36
]
.

Proposition 11

(Dirksen
[
36
]
, Theorem 5.5)
.

Let
F\subset L_{\psi_{2}}
. Given
1\leq p<\infty
, set
\ell:=\lfloor\log_{2}p\rfloor
and
k_{0}:=\min\{k>\ell:2^{k/2}>\sqrt{n}\}
. Let
(\mathcal{F}_{k})
be an optimal admissible sequence for
\gamma_{2,p}(F,\mathsf{d})
and, for any
f\in F
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\pi_{k}(f)\in\argmin_{f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}\mathsf{d}(f,f^{\prime})
. Then there exists universal constant
c>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
u\geq 2
, with probability at least
1-ce^{-pu/4}
,

(356)

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in F}\sup_{k\geq k_{0}}\left|A(\pi_{k}(f))\right|^{1/2}-\sup_{f\in F}\left|A(\pi_{k_{0}}(f))\right|^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{u}\left[25\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F,\mathsf{d})}{\sqrt{n}}+\left(85\frac{\bar{\Delta}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(F,\mathsf{d})}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1/2}\right].

Moreover, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
u\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-ce^{-pu/4}
,

(357)

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in F}\left|A(\pi_{k_{0}}(f))\right|^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{u}[4(1+\sqrt{2})+2]^{1/2}\bar{\Delta}(F).

Finally, we need two additional lemmas.

Lemma 11.10

(Lemma A.3 in Dirksen
[
36
]
)
.

Fix
1\leq p<\infty
, set
\ell:=\lfloor\log_{2}p\rfloor
and let
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be a finite collection of real-valued random variables with
|T|\leq 2^{2^{\ell}}
.

Then

(358)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}|X_{t}|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq 2\sup_{t\in T}(\mathbb{E}|X_{t}|^{p})^{1/p}.

Lemma 11.11

(Lemma A.5 in Dirksen
[
36
]
)
.

Fix
1\leq p<\infty
and
0<\alpha<\infty
. Let
\gamma\geq 0
and suppose that
\xi
is a positive random variable such that for some
c\geq 1
and
u_{*}>0
, for all
u\geq u_{*}
,

(359)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\xi>\gamma u)\leq c\exp(-pu^{\alpha}/4).

Then, for a constant
\tilde{c}_{\alpha}>0
, depending only on
\alpha
,

(360)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}\xi^{p})^{1/p}\leq\gamma(\tilde{c}_{\alpha}c+u_{*}).

Proof of Theorem
11.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{F}_{k})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{k})
be optimal admissible sequences for
\gamma_{2,p}(F)
and

\gamma_{2,p}(G)
respectively. Set
\ell:=\lfloor\log_{2}p\rfloor
,
k_{0}:=\min\{k>\ell:2^{k/2}>\sqrt{n}\}
and let us define

\mathcal{I}:=\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\ell<k<k_{0}\}

and

\mathcal{J}:=\{k\in\mathbb{N}:k\geq k_{0}\}
. Given
(f,g)\in F\times G
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we take the usual selections

\pi_{k}(f)\in\argmin_{f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mathsf{d}(f,f^{\prime}),

and

\Pi_{k}(g)\in\argmin_{g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{k}}\mathsf{d}(g,g^{\prime}).

For convenience, we also define

\mathcal{P}_{k}(f,g):=A(\pi_{k}(f),\Pi_{k}(g))
and

\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g):=\pi_{k}(f)\Pi_{k}(g)
. Our proof will rely on the chain:

(361)

\displaystyle A(f,g)-A(\pi_{\ell}(f),\Pi_{\ell}(g))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\left[\mathcal{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathcal{P}_{k}(f,g)\right]+\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{P}_{k}(f,g)-\mathcal{P}_{k-1}(f,g)\right],

where we have used that
\cup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_{k}\times\mathcal{G}_{k}
is dense on
F\times G
.

Fix
u\geq 2
. Given any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define the event

\Omega_{k,\mathcal{I},u,p}
for which, for all
f\in F
and
g\in G
, we have

(362)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{P}_{k}(f,g)-\mathcal{P}_{k-1}(f,g)|\leq 2(1+\sqrt{2})u2^{k/2}\frac{\|\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g)-\mathscr{P}_{k-1}(f,g)]\|_{\psi_{1}}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Define also the event
\Omega_{k,\mathcal{J},u,p}
for which, for all
f\in F
and
g\in G
, we have both inequalities:

(363)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{k+1}(f)-\pi_{k}(f)\|_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{u}2^{k/2}\frac{[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\|\pi_{k+1}(f)-\pi_{k}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}},

(364)

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{k+1}(g)-\Pi_{k}(g)\|_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{u}2^{k/2}\frac{[2(1+\sqrt{2})+1]^{1/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\|\Pi_{k+1}(g)-\Pi_{k}(g)\|_{\psi_{2}}.

By an union bound over all possible 4-tuples

(\pi_{k-1}(f),\pi_{k}(f),\Pi_{k-1}(g),\Pi_{k}(g))
we have
|\Omega_{k,\mathcal{I},u,p}|\leq|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}||\mathcal{F}_{k}||\mathcal{G}_{k}||\mathcal{G}_{k-1}|\leq 2^{2^{k+2}}
. If
\Omega_{\mathcal{I},u,p}:=\cap_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\Omega_{k,\mathcal{I},u,p}
, the first bound on Lemma
11.9
for
k\in\mathcal{I}
and Lemma A.4 in
[
36
]
(using that
k>\ell
over
\mathcal{I}
) imply that there is universal constant
c>0

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{I},u,p}^{c})\leq ce^{-pu/4}.

Similarly, the second bound in Lemma
11.9
for
k\in\mathcal{J}
and Lemma A.4 in
[
36
]
(using that
k>\ell
over
\mathcal{J}
) imply that for the event

\Omega_{\mathcal{J},u,p}:=\cap_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\Omega_{k,\mathcal{J},u,p}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{J},u,p}^{c})\leq ce^{-pu/4}.

We now also define the event
\Omega_{u,p}
as the intersection of
\Omega_{\mathcal{I},u,p}\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{J},u,p}
and the events for which both inequalities of Proposition
11
hold for both classes
F
and
G
. Clearly, by such proposition and the two previous displays we have

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{u,p}^{c})\leq ce^{-pu/4}
from an union bound.

We next fix
u\geq 2
and assume that
\Omega_{u,p}
always holds. We now bound the chain over
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
separately.

Subgaussian path
\mathcal{I}
.

From (
362
) and the inequality

(365)

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g)-\mathscr{P}_{k-1}(f,g)\|_{\psi_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi_{k}(f)-\pi_{k-1}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}\|\Pi_{k}(g)\|_{\psi_{2}}+\|\pi_{k-1}(f)\|_{\psi_{2}}\|\Pi_{k}(g)-\Pi_{k-1}(g)\|_{\psi_{2}}

(366)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\Delta}(G)[\mathsf{d}(f,\pi_{k}(f))+\mathsf{d}(f,\pi_{k-1}(f))]+\bar{\Delta}(F)[\mathsf{d}(g,\Pi_{k}(g))+\mathsf{d}(g,\Pi_{k-1}(g))]

implying

(367)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}[\mathcal{P}_{k}(f,g)-\mathcal{P}_{k-1}(f,g)]\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\sqrt{2})^{2}\frac{u}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\bar{\Delta}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(G)+\bar{\Delta}(G)\gamma_{2,p}(F)\right].

Subexponential path
\mathcal{J}
.

Note that
A(f,g)=\hat{\mathbf{P}}fg-\mathbf{P}(\hat{\mathbf{P}}fg)
and thus, by Jensen’s and triangle inequalities,

(368)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}[\mathcal{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathcal{P}_{k}(f,g)]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\hat{\mathbf{P}}[\mathscr{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g)]\right|+\left|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbf{P}\hat{\mathbf{P}}[\mathscr{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g)]\right|

(369)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\hat{\mathbf{P}}\left|\mathscr{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g)\right|+\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbf{P}\hat{\mathbf{P}}\left|\mathscr{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g)\right|.

Let us denote
\hat{T}_{k}:=\hat{\mathbf{P}}\left|\mathscr{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathscr{P}_{k}(f,g)\right|
. We have the split

(370)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\hat{\mathbf{P}}\pi_{k}(f)[\Pi_{k+1}(g)-\Pi_{k}(g)]\right|+\left|\hat{\mathbf{P}}\Pi_{k+1}(g)[\pi_{k+1}(f)-\pi_{k}(f)]\right|.

By Cauchy-Schwarz,

(371)

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\mathbf{P}}\pi_{k}(f)[\Pi_{k+1}(g)-\Pi_{k}(g)]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi_{k}(f)\|_{n}\|\Pi_{k+1}(g)-\Pi_{k}(g)]\|_{n}

(372)

\displaystyle\leq\left[\{A(\pi_{k}(f))\}^{1/2}+\{\mathbf{P}\pi_{k}^{2}(f)\}^{1/2}\right]\|\Pi_{k+1}(g)-\Pi_{k}(g)]\|_{n},

which together with (
364
), bounds in Proposition
11
and
\sqrt{u}\geq 1
give

(373)

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\mathbf{P}}\pi_{k}(f)[\Pi_{k+1}(g)-\Pi_{k}(g)]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{2}u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left[c_{1}\bar{\Delta}(F)+25\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}+\left\{\frac{85\bar{\Delta}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}\right\}^{1/2}\right]\mathsf{d}(\Pi_{k+1}(g),\Pi_{k}(g))

(374)

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\frac{u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(\Pi_{k+1}(g),\Pi_{k}(g))\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(F)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}\right],

by Young’s inequality and constants
c_{1}:=\{4(1+\sqrt{2}+2)\}^{1/2}+1
,
c_{2}:=\{2(1+\sqrt{2}+1)\}^{1/2}
,
c_{3}:=c_{1}+\frac{\sqrt{85}}{2}
and
c_{4}:=25+\frac{\sqrt{85}}{2}
.
An identical bound gives

(375)

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\mathbf{P}}\Pi_{k+1}(g)[\pi_{k+1}(f)-\pi_{k}(f)]\right|

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\frac{u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(\pi_{k+1}(f),\pi_{k}(f))\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(G)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{\sqrt{n}}\right].

We thus conclude that

(376)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\frac{u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(\Pi_{k+1}(g),\Pi_{k}(g))\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(F)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}\right]

(377)

\displaystyle+c_{2}\frac{u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(\pi_{k+1}(f),\pi_{k}(f))\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(G)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{\sqrt{n}}\right].

Note that, in fact, we have proved that the above bound on
\hat{T}_{k}
holds with probability at least
1-c\exp(-pu/4)
for any
u\geq 2
. Thus, from Lemma
11.11
we have, for some universal constant
c_{0}>0
,

(378)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\hat{T}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}c_{2}\frac{u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(\Pi_{k+1}(g),\Pi_{k}(g))\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(F)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}\right]

(379)

\displaystyle+c_{0}c_{2}\frac{u2^{k/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathsf{d}(\pi_{k+1}(f),\pi_{k}(f))\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(G)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{\sqrt{n}}\right].

Using the previous two bounds in (
369
) gives, after using the triangle inequality for
\mathsf{d}
, summing over
k\in\mathcal{J}
and using the definition of
\gamma_{2,p}(F)
and
\gamma_{2,p}(G)
(recalling that
k>\ell
),

(380)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}[\mathcal{P}_{k+1}(f,g)-\mathcal{P}_{k}(f,g)]\right|

\displaystyle\leq(1+c_{0})(1+2^{-1/2})c_{2}\frac{u}{\sqrt{n}}\gamma_{2,p}(G)\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(F)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}\right]

(381)

\displaystyle+(1+c_{0})(1+2^{-1/2})c_{2}\frac{u}{\sqrt{n}}\gamma_{2,p}(F)\left[c_{3}\bar{\Delta}(G)+c_{4}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{\sqrt{n}}\right].

From the above bound, (
367
) and (
361
) we conclude that, for any
u\geq 2
, on the event
\Omega_{u,p}
of probability at least
1-e^{-pu/4}
, we have

(382)

\displaystyle\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}|A(f,g)|^{1/2}-\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}|A(\pi_{\ell}(f),\Pi_{\ell}(g))|^{1/2}

(383)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{u}\left[\frac{c_{5}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\bar{\Delta}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(G)+\bar{\Delta}(G)\gamma_{2,p}(F)\right)+c_{6}\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{n}\right]^{1/2},

with
c_{5}:=2(1+\sqrt{2}^{2}+(1+c_{0})(1+2^{-1/2}))c_{2}c_{3}
and

c_{6}:=2(1+c_{0})c_{2}c_{4}(1+2^{-1/2})
. This and Lemma
11.11
(with
\alpha=2
) imply that

(384)

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.72218pt\,\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}|A(f,g)|^{1/2}-\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}|A(\pi_{\ell}(f),\Pi_{\ell}(g))|^{1/2}\,\kern-1.72218pt\right|}_{p}

(385)

\displaystyle\leq c\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\bar{\Delta}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(G)+\bar{\Delta}(G)\gamma_{2,p}(F)\right)+\frac{\gamma_{2,p}(F)\gamma_{2,p}(G)}{n}\right]^{1/2}.

We also have from Lemma
11.10
,

(386)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}|A(\pi_{\ell}(f),\Pi_{\ell}(g))|^{p/2}\right)^{2/p}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}\left(\mathbb{E}|A(\pi_{\ell}(f),\Pi_{\ell}(g))|^{p/2}\right)^{2/p}

(387)

\displaystyle\leq c\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}\left[\|\mathscr{P}_{\ell}(f,g)-\mathbf{P}\mathscr{P}_{\ell}(f,g)\|_{\psi_{1}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}+\frac{p}{n}\right)\right],

where the second inequality follows from Bernstein’s inequality for
A(\pi_{\ell}(f),\Pi_{\ell}(g))
and Lemma A.2 in
[
36
]
. The two previous displays finish the proof.
∎

11.4
Oracle inequalities with
\delta
-uniform subgaussian optimal rates

Oracle inequalities
provide a statistical guarantee on the prediction risk for
miss-specified
models
[
10
]
. In the high-dimensional setting, a
n
-sized iid measurement of
y=f+\xi
is represented in vector form by

(388)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{f}+\boldsymbol{\xi},

where one does
not
have the condition
\boldsymbol{f}=\mathbb{X}\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
with
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
sparse. Similarly to estimation rates in the well-specified case, it is of practical relevance to obtain optimal oracle inequalities valid for estimators tuned
independently
of the failure probability

\delta
. Oracle inequalities with the optimal
subgaussian
rate
\sqrt{s\log(ep/s)/n}+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}
are of preference. See item (c) of Section
1
for related discussion.

It was shown for the first time in Bellec et al.
[
7
]
that the Lasso or Slope estimators can be tuned independently of
\delta
; see Theorems 4.2 and 6.1 and Corollaries 4.3 and 6.2 in that paper. This is valid with subgaussian data with noise independent of features. In Proposition 3.2, it is also shown in the Gaussian setting that Lasso indeed satisfies an oracle inequality with the subgaussian rate

\sqrt{s\log p/n}+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}
, improving on previous non-subgaussian rates of the form
\sqrt{s\log(p/\delta)/n}
. For better appreciation, we recall their Theorem 6.1 for the Slope estimator

(389)

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}\in

\displaystyle\argmin_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{b}\rangle\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\sharp},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
denotes the Slope norm with the sequence
\bar{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=(\bar{\omega}_{j})_{j\in[p]}
given by

\bar{\omega}_{j}:=\log(2p/j)
.
We restate their result up to absolute constants using our notation and assuming the standard normalization assumption of the design matrix columns. Using the notation in
[
7
]
, we define

\bar{\Omega}_{s}:=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}^{2}},

and

\mathcal{C}_{\WRE}(s,c_{0}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\sharp}\leq(1+c_{0})\bar{\Omega}_{s}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\right\}.

Given a convex cone
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we define

\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbb{C}):=\sup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{C}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}}{\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}}.

Theorem 11.12

(Theorem 6.1 in
[
7
]
)
.

Take tuning

\lambda\asymp\sigma/\sqrt{n}.

Let
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta_{0}
, for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
s\in[p]
and all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}=s
,

(390)

\displaystyle\lambda\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\sharp}+\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}+C(s,\delta_{0})\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}^{2}}{n}.

In above,

(391)

\displaystyle C(s,\delta_{0})\asymp\hat{\mu}^{2}(s)\bigvee\frac{\log(1/\delta_{0})}{s\log(2p/s)},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hat{\mu}(s):=\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\WRE}(s,7)).

The tuning of the above oracle inequality is
independent
of
\delta
with the optimal rate

\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}^{2}/n\asymp s\log(2ep/s)/n:=r_{n}^{2}.

This rate is the optimal
subgaussian
rate, but the fundamentally assume the noise is independent of features. Considering the previous observations, it still an open question if either
\delta
-uniform oracle inequalities with the optimal subgaussian rate can be achieved for Lasso or Slope when the noise is dependent on the features. We next present such type of results for Lasso, Slope and nuclear norm regularization. The main tool we use is the new concentration inequality for the
Multiplier Process
proven in Section
11.2
. The following oracle inequalities are valid even when
\xi
might depend on the feature vector
\boldsymbol{x}
, as long as

\mathbb{E}[\xi\boldsymbol{x}]=0
. This condition is satisfied, for example, in the least-squares statistical learning framework where

(392)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{b}^{*}\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{b}}\mathbb{E}[(y-\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{b}\rangle)^{2}],

i.e.
y=f+\xi
with
f=\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{b}^{*}\rangle
and
\mathbb{E}[\xi\boldsymbol{x}]=0
. In this particular case, miss-specification occurs when
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
is not exactly sparse.

Theorem 11.13

(Slope)
.

Grant Assumption
3
and assume that
\mathbb{E}[\xi\boldsymbol{x}]=0
. Let
\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
as in Theorem
4.1
. Recall definition of the cone
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6)
in Remark
5
. Take tuning
\lambda\asymp\sigma L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\sqrt{n}
.

Then there are universal constant
c_{1}>0
,
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that the following holds. Given any
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

(393)

\displaystyle\delta\geq\exp\left(-c_{1}\frac{n}{L^{4}}\right),

with probability at least
1-\delta
,
for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
s\in[p]
and all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq s
,

(394)

\displaystyle\lambda\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\sharp}+\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{b})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}+C(s)\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}^{2}}{n}+C_{2}\sigma^{2}L^{2}\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{n}.

In above,

(395)

\displaystyle C(s):=C_{1}\sigma^{2}L^{2}\rho_{1}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\hat{\mu}^{2}(s),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hat{\mu}(s):=\hat{\mu}_{n}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6)).

We note that
C(s)
is independent of
\delta
. For simplicity, consider the normalized with case
\sigma=L=1
and
\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\leq 1
. Then the rate in the oracle inequality above is the subgaussian rate

C(s)\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}^{2}}{n}+C_{2}\sigma^{2}L^{2}\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\asymp\hat{\mu}^{2}(s)\frac{s\log(2ep/s)}{n}+\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{n}.

An analogous oracle inequality holds for Lasso.

Theorem 11.14

(Lasso)
.

Grant Assumption
3
and assume that
\mathbb{E}[\xi\boldsymbol{x}]=0
. Let
\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
as in Theorem
4.1
. Recall the cone
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{b},\|\cdot\|_{1}}(6)
for any
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
in Definition
8.6
. Take tuning

\lambda\asymp\sigma L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\log p/n}
.

Then there are universal constant
c_{1}>0
,
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that the following holds. Given any
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

(396)

\displaystyle\delta\geq\exp\left(-c_{1}\frac{n}{L^{4}}\right),

with probability at least
1-\delta
,
for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
s\in[p]
and all

\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq s
,

(397)

\displaystyle\lambda\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{b}\|_{1}+\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}(\boldsymbol{b})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}+C(\boldsymbol{b})\frac{s\log p}{n}+C_{2}\sigma^{2}L^{2}\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{n}.

In above,

(398)

\displaystyle C(\boldsymbol{b}):=C_{1}\sigma^{2}L^{2}\rho_{1}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\hat{\mu}^{2}(\boldsymbol{b}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hat{\mu}(\boldsymbol{b}):=\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{b},\|\cdot\|_{1}}(6)).

Consider now the estimator

(399)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{B}}\in

\displaystyle\argmin_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{B}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\mathbf{B}\|_{N}.

Recall that
\mathfrak{X}
is the design operator with coordinates
\mathfrak{X}_{i}(\mathbf{B}):=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{B}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle
. We can state an
\delta
-uniform oracle inequality for
\hat{\mathbf{B}}
with optimal subgaussian rate for low-rank miss-specified models.

Theorem 11.15

(Nuclear norm)
.

Grant Assumption
3
and assume that
\mathbb{E}[\xi\mathbf{X}]=0
. Let
\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
as in Theorem
4.2
. Recall the cone
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\|\cdot\|_{N}}(6)
for any
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
in Definition
8.6
. Take tuning
\lambda\asymp\sigma L\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})(\sqrt{d_{1}/n}+\sqrt{d_{2}/n})
.

Then there are universal constant
c_{1}>0
,
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that the following holds. Given any
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

(400)

\displaystyle\delta\geq\exp\left(-c_{1}\frac{n}{L^{4}}\right),

with probability at least
1-\delta
,
for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
r\in\{1,\ldots,d_{1}\wedge d_{2}\}
and all

\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
with
\rank(\mathbf{B})\leq r
,

(401)

\displaystyle\lambda\|\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\|_{N}+\frac{1}{n}\|\mathfrak{X}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\|\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{F}^{2}+C(\mathbf{B})\frac{rd_{1}+rd_{2}}{n}+C_{2}\sigma^{2}L^{2}\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{n}.

In above,

(402)

\displaystyle C(\mathbf{B}):=C_{1}\sigma^{2}L^{2}\rho_{N}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\hat{\mu}^{2}(\mathbf{B}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hat{\mu}(\mathbf{B}):=\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\|\cdot\|_{N}}(6)).

We only prove Theorem
11.13
as the proof of Theorems
11.14
and
11.15
follow similar argument. Next,
\MP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2})
has the analogous definition in Definition
8.1
when

\boldsymbol{u}\equiv 0
.

Lemma 11.16

(Dimension reduction)
.

Suppose

(i)

(\mathbb{X},\boldsymbol{\xi})
satisfies the
\MP_{\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2})

for some positive numbers
\mathsf{f}_{1}
and
\mathsf{f}_{2}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{X}
satisfies the
\TP_{\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{c}_{1};\mathsf{c}_{2})
for some positive numbers

\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2}
.

(iii)

\lambda\geq 2[\mathsf{f}_{2}+(\nicefrac{{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{2}}}{{\mathsf{c}_{1}}})].

Then, for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
s\in[p]
and all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq s
, either

(403)

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{b})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2},

or
\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{b}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6)
or

(404)

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}\leq 2\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})_{i}^{\sharp}\leq\frac{4\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

Proof.

Using
\boldsymbol{y}^{(n)}=\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}+\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}
, the first order condition of (
389
) at

\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}
implies: there exist

\boldsymbol{v}\in\partial\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}\|_{\sharp}
such that for all
\boldsymbol{b}
and
\boldsymbol{\Delta}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{b}
,

(405)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)},\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\rangle\leq\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\rangle-\lambda\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\Delta}\rangle.

Suppose that
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq s
. Next we use that

-\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\leq\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}\|_{\sharp}
. This fact, items (i)-(iii) and proceeding as in the chain of inequalities in (
72
), entail

(406)

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}-\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}),\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}+(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{\sharp}+\lambda\big(\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}\|_{\sharp}\big)

(407)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}+\triangle,

where, in last inequality, we used Lemma
8.5
(since
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq s
) with
\nu:=1/2
and defined

(408)

\displaystyle\triangle:=(\nicefrac{{3\lambda}}{{2}})\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})_{j}^{\sharp}-(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\sum_{j=s+1}^{p}\bar{\omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})_{j}^{\sharp}=2\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})_{j}^{\sharp}-(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{\sharp}.

Moreover, from the parallelogram law,

(409)

\displaystyle I:=\langle\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)},\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{b})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

If
I\leq 0
then the statement of the lemma holds trivially. Next, we assume that
I\geq 0
.

Define
G:=\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}
and
H:=(\nicefrac{{3\lambda}}{{2}})\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})_{i}^{\sharp}
. We split the argument in two cases.

Case 1:

\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}G\leq H
. In that case, from (
407
) and
I\geq 0
we obtain

\boldsymbol{\Delta}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6)
.

Case 2:

\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}G\geq H
. In that case we obtain
G^{2}\leq\frac{2\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}G\Rightarrow G\leq\frac{2\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}
. Therefore
H\leq 2\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}
.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 11.17
.

Suppose that items (i)-(iii) in Lemma
11.16
hold.

Then, for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
s\in[p]
and all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq s
,

(410)

\displaystyle\lambda\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\sharp}+\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{b})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}+\left[\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}+2\lambda\bar{\Omega}_{s}\hat{\mu}_{n}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6))\right]^{2}\bigvee\frac{28\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Delta}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}-\boldsymbol{b}
and define
\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{v})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}
. From (
407
), (
409
) and definition of
\triangle
,

(411)

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{\sharp}+\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})\leq\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{b})-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}+2\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})_{j}^{\sharp}.

Recall the 3 cases in Lemma
11.16
. The 1st is

\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})\leq\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{b})
which implies the theorem trivially.

The 2nd case is when
\boldsymbol{\Delta}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6)
. For simplicity, let
\hat{\mu}_{s}:=\hat{\mu}_{n}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6))
. By decomposability and Cauchy-Schwarz,

(412)

\displaystyle 2\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bar{\omega}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})_{j}^{\sharp}\leq 2\lambda\bar{\Omega}_{s}\hat{\mu}_{s}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}.

From (
411
), (
412
)
and Young’s inequality

\left[\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}+2\lambda\bar{\Omega}\hat{\mu}_{s}\right]\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}+2\lambda\bar{\Omega}_{s}\hat{\mu}_{s}\right]^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta})\|_{2}^{2},

we get

(413)

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{\sharp}+\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})\leq\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{b})+\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}+2\lambda\bar{\Omega}_{s}\hat{\mu}_{s}\right]^{2}.

Finally, the 3rd case is when the bounds (
404
) hold. In that case, (
411
) implies

(414)

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}\|_{\sharp}+\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{b}})\leq\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{b})+\frac{2\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}+\frac{8\mathsf{f}_{1}^{2}}{3\mathsf{c}_{1}^{2}}.

The proof is complete by noting that the bound in the statement of the theorem is larger than the two previous displays.
∎

The Proof of Theorem
11.13
now follows from the previous theorem and the probabilistic guarantees in Section
8.3
for
L
-subgaussian designs.

Proof of Theorem
11.13
.

Proposition E.2 in
[
7
]
implies

\mathscr{G}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbb{B}_{\sharp})\lesssim\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
.

From Proposition
3
, assuming

(415)

\displaystyle\delta\geq\exp\left(-c_{1}\frac{n}{L^{4}}\right),

for large enough universal
c_{1}>0
, it follows that for universal
\mathsf{c}_{1}\in(0,1)
and

\mathsf{c}_{2}\asymp L\frac{\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}{\sqrt{n}},

on an event
\Omega_{1}
of probability at least
1-\delta/2
, property

\TP_{\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{c}_{1};\mathsf{c}_{2})
is satisfied.

From Proposition
5
(with
\delta=\rho
), by enlarging
c_{1}
if necessary, if we take

\mathsf{f}_{1}\asymp\sigma L\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathsf{f}_{2}\asymp\sigma L\frac{\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}{\sqrt{n}},

we have that on an event

\Omega_{2}
of probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

\MP_{\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{f}_{1};\mathsf{f}_{2})
is satisfied.

Together with definition of
\lambda
, an union bound implies that all conditions (i)-(iii) in Theorem
11.17
are met and Theorem
11.13
follows.
∎
